/>

<Callout type="definition" title="The chain, in one line">
  Saratoga (1777) → proof the Americans could win → French alliance (1778) → French money, troops, and navy → Britain fights a world war → Yorktown (1781), where the French fleet made the trap work → Treaty of Paris (1783).
</Callout>

## 4.6 Why did the underdog win?

A favorite causation question. Britain had the bigger army, the best navy, and far more money. It lost anyway.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-accent-900 dark:text-accent-100 mb-3">American advantages</h3>
    <ul class="space-y-2 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">
      <li>⭐ <strong>Washington's leadership:</strong> he kept an army in existence, which was enough</li>
      <li>🗺️ <strong>Home ground:</strong> familiar land and huge territory to retreat into</li>
      <li>🔥 <strong>Motivation:</strong> fighting for their own homes and ideals</li>
      <li>♻️ <strong>Resilience:</strong> they could lose battles (and did) and keep fighting</li>
      <li>⚜️ <strong>Foreign help:</strong> the French alliance, above all the French navy</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-3">British problems</h3>
    <ul class="space-y-2 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">
      <li>🚢 <strong>Distance:</strong> 3,000 miles of ocean between London and the army</li>
      <li>📦 <strong>Long supply lines</strong> for every musket ball and biscuit</li>
      <li>🌲 <strong>Too much land:</strong> they could take cities but never control the countryside</li>
      <li>🌍 <strong>A world war</strong> after 1778: France, Spain, and the Dutch to fight too</li>
      <li>💷 <strong>Political cost:</strong> the war grew unpopular and expensive at home</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
</div>

## 4.7 The Treaty of Paris, 1783

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-4 gap-3">
  <TremorCard label="Term 1" value="Independence" helper="Britain recognizes the United States as an independent nation." />
  <TremorCard label="Term 2" value="West to the Mississippi" helper="The Mississippi River becomes the western boundary: the new nation is huge." />
  <TremorCard label="Term 3" value="Fishing rights" helper="Americans may fish off the coast of Canada (Newfoundland)." />
  <TremorCard label="Term 4" value="Debts + Loyalists" helper="Americans will pay debts owed to British merchants and honor Loyalist claims for confiscated property." />
</div>

<Callout type="warning" title="Two treaties, one name">
  <strong>1763</strong> Treaty of Paris: ends the French and Indian War, Britain takes Canada and Florida. <strong>1783</strong> Treaty of Paris: ends the Revolution, Britain recognizes independence. Also notice that Term 4 was largely ignored by the states, which Britain later used as an excuse to keep its forts on the western frontier (Section 8).
</Callout>

# Section 5 · Revolutionary Ideals, Unevenly Applied

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl overflow-hidden bg-gradient-to-br from-ink-950 via-accent-950 to-ink-900 px-6 py-10 text-center">
  <p class="font-mono text-white/60 text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.25em] mb-3">AMSCO Ch. 5 · Topic 3.6</p>
  <h2 class="font-serif text-3xl md:text-5xl font-bold text-white mb-2">Who Did Liberty Include?</h2>
  <p class="text-white/80 max-w-xl mx-auto">The Revolution changed American society, but not equally. The distinction between change and continuity is the whole point of this topic.</p>
</div>

## 5.1 New state governments

While the war raged, the former colonies wrote constitutions; ten had done it by 1777. Conservatives wanted law and order, liberals wanted protection against tyranny, and almost every state ended up with the same four features:

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-4 gap-3">
  <TremorCard label="Feature 1" value="A bill of rights" helper="Each constitution opened with a list of rights, like jury trial and freedom of religion, that officials could not violate." />
  <TremorCard label="Feature 2" value="Separation of powers" helper="An elected legislature (usually two houses), an elected governor, and courts. A safeguard against a too-powerful executive." />
  <TremorCard label="Feature 3" value="Property to vote" helper="Voting for white men who owned some property, on the theory that owners had a bigger stake in government." />
  <TremorCard label="Feature 4" value="More property to serve" helper="Candidates for office had to meet a higher property requirement than voters." />
</div>

## 5.2 Who gained what?

Each card shows what changed (red) and what stayed the same (black). Essays reward you for naming both.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-3">👩 Women</h3>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Changed</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-3">Ran farms and businesses while men fought; powered the boycotts (Daughters of Liberty); served as nurses and cooks; a few fought, like Mary McCauley ("Molly Pitcher") at Monmouth and Deborah Sampson, disguised as a man. <strong>Republican Motherhood</strong> made women's education matter.</p>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mb-1">Stayed the same</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Still second-class: no vote (New Jersey's brief exception ended in 1807), and married women still could not own property in their own names.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-3">✊🏾 African Americans</h3>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Changed</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-3">About 5,000 fought as Patriots; thousands won freedom behind British lines. Northern states began ending slavery, Congress stopped the importation of enslaved people during the war, and some southern owners freed the people they enslaved.</p>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mb-1">Stayed the same</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Slavery survived and grew in the South, where owners built new justifications for it. Free Black Americans still faced discrimination everywhere.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-3">🪶 Native Americans</h3>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Changed</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-3">For the worse: Britain's defeat removed the one power that had tried to limit settlement, and the 1783 treaty handed their lands to the U.S. without consulting them.</p>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mb-1">Stayed the same</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Constant pressure on their land from settlers, now backed by a new nation hungry for the West.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-3">⛪ Religion</h3>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Changed</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-3">Most states separated church and state; the Anglican Church lost its tax support in the South. Jefferson's <strong>Virginia Statute for Religious Freedom</strong> (1786) became the model.</p>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mb-1">Stayed the same</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">New Hampshire, Connecticut, and Massachusetts kept taxes supporting the Congregational Church for decades (Massachusetts until 1833).</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-3">🎩 Aristocracy</h3>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Changed</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-3">Titles of nobility banned; <strong>primogeniture</strong> (the eldest son inherits everything) abolished; big Loyalist estates confiscated, split up, and sold.</p>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mb-1">Stayed the same</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Wealthy elites still dominated politics, and great economic inequality remained.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-3">🧑‍🌾 Ordinary white men</h3>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Changed</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-3">Written constitutions with bills of rights, more elected offices, and a republican culture that valued the common citizen.</p>
    <p class="text-xs font-bold uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mb-1">Stayed the same</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Voting still required property, and holding office required even more.</p>
  </div>
</div>

## 5.3 "Remember the Ladies"

In March 1776, while John Adams sat in the Continental Congress, his wife Abigail wrote to him from Massachusetts. Read the exchange; it is a favorite stimulus.

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="Abigail Adams to John Adams, March 31, 1776">
  I desire you would Remember the Ladies, and be more generous and favourable to them than your ancestors. Do not put such unlimited power into the hands of the Husbands.
</SpeechBubble>

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="John Adams to Abigail Adams, April 14, 1776">
  Depend upon it, We know better than to repeal our Masculine systems.
</SpeechBubble>

<Callout type="insight" title="Republican Motherhood: change inside continuity">
  After the war, a new ideal gave women a <strong>political job without political rights</strong>: raise sons to be virtuous republican citizens. Because a mother had to teach virtue, more people argued that girls deserved real education. That is change (education, respect) wrapped in continuity (no vote, domestic role). If an essay asks how the Revolution affected women, this is your best evidence.
</Callout>

## 5.4 Slavery's first retreats, and its limits

The Revolution's language of liberty pushed several northern states to end slavery, almost always **gradually** (children born after a certain date would be freed as adults). Plot the dates, then notice what is missing: no southern state appears.

<NumberLine
  title="Northern states begin ending slavery"
  min={1775}
  max={1805}
  step={5}
  marks={[
    { at: 1777, label: "Vermont constitution", color: "#b91c1c" },
    { at: 1780, label: "Pennsylvania (gradual)", color: "#b91c1c" },
    { at: 1783, label: "Massachusetts (court ruling)", color: "#b91c1c" },
    { at: 1784, label: "Connecticut + Rhode Island", color: "#b91c1c" },
    { at: 1787, label: "Northwest Ordinance bans it in the territory", color: "#27272a" },
    { at: 1799, label: "New York (gradual)", color: "#b91c1c" },
    { at: 1804, label: "New Jersey (gradual)", color: "#b91c1c" }
  ]}
  intervals={[{ from: 1777, to: 1804, label: "Northern gradual emancipation", color: "#b91c1c" }]}
/>

<Callout type="note" title="Why 'gradual' matters">
  Gradual emancipation laws freed no one immediately. Pennsylvania's 1780 law, for example, freed children born after it passed, and only once they reached age 28. In the South, slavery was growing more economically important, not less, especially once the cotton gin arrived in 1793 (Section 10).
</Callout>

## 5.5 A revolution heard around the world

The American Revolution proved that Enlightenment ideas could overthrow an empire, and the world noticed.

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="France · 1789" value="French Revolution" helper="French officers who fought in America (like Lafayette) brought the ideas home. France's war spending also bankrupted the crown." />
  <TremorCard label="Haiti · 1791-1804" value="Haitian Revolution" helper="Enslaved people in the French colony of Saint-Domingue rose up and founded Haiti, the first nation born from a slave revolt." />
  <TremorCard label="Latin America · 1810s-1820s" value="Independence wars" helper="Spanish colonies from Mexico to Argentina broke away, citing the same ideas of natural rights and self-government." />
</div>

    {

        "the return of land taken under the Proclamation of 1763"

      answer: 2,

    }
  ]}
/>

# Section 6 · The Articles of Confederation

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl overflow-hidden bg-gradient-to-br from-ink-950 via-accent-950 to-ink-900 px-6 py-10 text-center">
  <p class="font-mono text-white/60 text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.25em] mb-3">AMSCO Ch. 5 · Topic 3.7</p>
  <h2 class="font-serif text-3xl md:text-5xl font-bold text-white mb-2">Weak on Purpose</h2>
  <p class="text-white/80 max-w-xl mx-auto">Americans had just fought a war against a powerful central government. So their first national government was designed to be too weak to become a tyranny. It worked, a little too well.</p>
</div>

## 6.1 The structure

John Dickinson drafted the Articles in 1776; Congress adopted them in **1777**, but they were not **ratified until 1781** because Maryland refused to sign until states like Virginia and New York gave up their claims to western land. Here is the whole national government:

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
  <div class="grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4 items-stretch">
    <div class="md:col-span-1 rounded-xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-4 text-center flex flex-col justify-center">
      <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-800 dark:text-accent-300">The only branch</p>
      <p class="font-serif text-2xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 my-1">Congress</p>
      <p class="text-xs text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">One house (unicameral) · one vote per state · 9 of 13 states to pass important laws · all 13 to amend</p>
    </div>
    <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-dashed border-ink-300 dark:border-ink-600 p-4 text-center flex flex-col justify-center opacity-80">
      <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500">Missing</p>
      <p class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-ink-500 my-1">No president</p>
      <p class="text-xs text-ink-500">Nobody to enforce the laws Congress passed</p>
    </div>
    <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-dashed border-ink-300 dark:border-ink-600 p-4 text-center flex flex-col justify-center opacity-80">
      <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500">Missing</p>
      <p class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-ink-500 my-1">No national courts</p>
      <p class="text-xs text-ink-500">No system to settle disputes or interpret national law</p>
    </div>
  </div>
  <div class="mt-4 flex flex-wrap justify-center gap-1.5 text-center" aria-label="The thirteen states">
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">NH</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">MA</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">RI</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">CT</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">NY</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">NJ</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">PA</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">DE</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">MD</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">VA</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">NC</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">SC</span>
    <span class="w-10 rounded-md bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-100 text-white dark:text-ink-900 text-[11px] font-bold py-1.5">GA</span>
  </div>
  <p class="mt-3 text-xs text-center text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300">The real power: thirteen states that kept their own taxes, trade rules, currencies, and militias. The Articles called their union "a firm league of friendship."</p>
</div>

## 6.2 What Congress could and could not do

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-3">✅ Could</h3>
    <ul class="space-y-2 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">
      <li>✅ Wage war</li>
      <li>✅ Make treaties</li>
      <li>✅ Send diplomats</li>
      <li>✅ Borrow money</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-accent-900 dark:text-accent-100 mb-3">❌ Could not</h3>
    <ul class="space-y-2 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">
      <li>❌ <strong>Collect taxes</strong> (it could only ask the states for money)</li>
      <li>❌ <strong>Regulate commerce</strong>, between states or with other nations</li>
      <li>❌ <strong>Enforce</strong> its laws (no executive) or force states to comply</li>
      <li>❌ <strong>Amend</strong> itself without all thirteen states agreeing</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
</div>

## 6.3 Real accomplishments

Do not write "the Articles accomplished nothing." That is an easy way to lose a point. The Articles government won the war, negotiated the Treaty of Paris, and passed two laws that shaped the whole West.

### The Land Ordinance of 1785

Congress had millions of acres of western land and no taxing power, so it decided to survey the land in a grid and sell it. Watch the surveyors' lines draw a **township**: 6 miles on each side, divided into 36 one-square-mile sections of 640 acres each. The law reserved **section 16 of every township for public schools**.

### The Northwest Ordinance of 1787

The Articles' greatest achievement. It turned the territory between the **Ohio River and the Great Lakes** into a ladder to statehood that every later territory would climb.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">Step 1</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Territory</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Congress appoints a governor, a secretary, and three judges.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">Step 2 · 5,000 free adult men</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Self-government</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Settlers elect their own territorial legislature and send a nonvoting delegate to Congress.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-800 dark:text-accent-300 mb-1">Step 3 · 60,000 people</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Statehood</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Write a constitution and join the Union <strong>on an equal footing</strong> with the original states.</p></div>
</div>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="Slavery" value="Prohibited" helper="'There shall be neither slavery nor involuntary servitude in the said territory.' A line between free and slave land decades before the Civil War." />
  <TremorCard label="Education" value="Encouraged" helper="'Schools and the means of education shall forever be encouraged.' Plus guarantees like trial by jury and religious freedom." />
  <TremorCard label="Future states" value="5 (+ part of MN)" helper="Ohio (1803), Indiana (1816), Illinois (1818), Michigan (1837), and Wisconsin (1848)." />
</div>

<Callout type="note" title="The social changes of the same years">
  Your notes also credit the Articles years with abolishing nobility and separating church and state. Those changes were real, but they happened mostly through <strong>state</strong> constitutions and laws (Section 5), not acts of Congress. Knowing which level of government did it is the kind of precision that separates a 4 from a 5.
</Callout>

## 6.4 Three kinds of trouble

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-accent-900 dark:text-accent-100 mb-2">💸 Financial</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">War debts went unpaid because Congress could only <strong>request</strong> money, and states rarely sent it. Congress and the states printed nearly worthless paper money. Credit dried up and a depression followed.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">🌍 Foreign</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">European nations had little respect for a country that could not pay its debts. Britain <strong>kept its forts</strong> on the western frontier and restricted American trade; Spain threatened American access to the Mississippi.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-400 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">🏠 Domestic</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">States taxed each other's goods with tariffs and fought over boundaries. And in 1786, farmers in western Massachusetts took up arms against their own state.</p>
  </div>
</div>

## 6.5 Shays' Rebellion: you are the Confederation Congress

Play the crisis that ended the Articles, then read what actually happened.

<BranchingScenario
  title="New York City, autumn 1786"
  context="You are a member of the Confederation Congress. In western Massachusetts, Daniel Shays, a Revolutionary War veteran, leads hundreds of farmers who are drowning in debt and high state taxes and furious that debtors are being thrown in jail. They have shut down the courts that foreclose on farms. Nearby sits the federal arsenal at Springfield, full of muskets. Massachusetts is asking for help."
  historicalAnswer="Congress voted in October 1786 to raise troops (officially to fight Native Americans) but, unable to tax, it could not pay them, and the states sent almost nothing. Massachusetts handled it alone: Boston merchants privately funded a state militia army, and in January 1787 militiamen drove the rebels from the Springfield arsenal with cannon fire, killing four. The national government had watched, helpless. Leaders like Washington, Madison, and Hamilton concluded the Articles had to be replaced, and within months delegates were meeting in Philadelphia."
  tree={{
    start: "opening",
    nodes: {
      opening: {
        scenario: "The rebels are drilling. The Springfield arsenal is guarded by a handful of men. Massachusetts wants federal troops. What does Congress do?",
        choices: [
          { text: "Raise a national army to protect the arsenal", next: "army" },
          { text: "Order the states to send money and soldiers", next: "order" },
          { text: "Leave it to Massachusetts", next: "leave" }
        ]
      },
      army: {
        scenario: "You vote to raise troops. Now you need money to pay them, and under the Articles Congress cannot tax. All you can do is send a request (a requisition) to the thirteen states.",
        choices: [
          { text: "Send the requisition and wait", next: "wait" },
          { text: "Borrow the money from Dutch bankers", next: "borrow" }
        ]
      },
      wait: {
        ending: "The states, broke and suspicious of one another, send almost nothing. Your army exists mostly on paper. Winter comes and the rebels move on Springfield while Congress watches.",
        historicalNote: "This is close to what happened in 1786: Congress authorized troops but could not fund them."
      },
      borrow: {
        ending: "The lenders ask how you will repay them. You cannot tax, the states ignore your requests, and you have not paid back the war loans you already owe. No new loan arrives in time.",
        historicalNote: "The national government was already in default on much of its war debt. Its credit was nearly worthless."
      },
      order: {
        ending: "You have no power to order the states to do anything. Your 'order' is a request, and there is no executive and no court to enforce it. A few states reply politely; none send soldiers.",
        historicalNote: "Under the Articles, Congress could not force states to comply with its requests. That weakness is the heart of the problem."
      },
      leave: {
        ending: "Massachusetts cannot pay its own militia either, so wealthy Boston merchants chip in to fund an army. It scatters the rebels at the Springfield arsenal. Order returns, but everyone noticed that the national government did nothing at all.",
        historicalNote: "This is essentially what happened, and the lesson hit hard: a nation that cannot protect its own arsenal is barely a nation."
      }
    }
  }}
/>

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="George Washington, letter to Henry Lee, October 1786">
  I am mortified beyond expression when I view the clouds that have spread over the brightest morn that ever dawned upon any country.
</SpeechBubble>

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="Thomas Jefferson, in Paris, letter to James Madison, January 1787">
  I hold it that a little rebellion now and then is a good thing, and as necessary in the political world as storms in the physical.
</SpeechBubble>

<Callout type="definition" title="The causal chain">
  Weak Articles (no taxing power) → unpaid debts and high state taxes → Shays' Rebellion (1786-87) → the national government cannot respond → leaders call for a stronger government → Constitutional Convention (1787). Notice Jefferson's calmer view: he was in France and trusted the people more than most of his fellow founders did. Keep that in mind when he leads the opposition party in the 1790s.
</Callout>

# Section 7 · Building the Constitution

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl overflow-hidden bg-gradient-to-br from-ink-950 via-accent-950 to-ink-900 px-6 py-10 text-center">
  <p class="font-mono text-white/60 text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.25em] mb-3">AMSCO Ch. 6 · Topics 3.8 + 3.9</p>
  <h2 class="font-serif text-3xl md:text-5xl font-bold text-white mb-2">The Constitutional Convention</h2>
  <p class="text-white/80 max-w-xl mx-auto">The delegates' problem in one sentence: build a government strong enough to work, without building another tyranny.</p>
</div>

## 7.1 The road to Philadelphia

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">1785 · Mount Vernon</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">A meeting at Washington's home</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Delegates agree interstate trade problems are serious enough to discuss again, with every state invited.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">1786 · Annapolis</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Only five states show up</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1"><strong>James Madison</strong> and <strong>Alexander Hamilton</strong> persuade them to call a bigger convention in Philadelphia to revise the Articles.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-800 dark:text-accent-300 mb-1">May to Sept 1787 · Philadelphia</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">"Revise" becomes "replace"</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Called only to revise the Articles, the nationalists quickly take control and write an entirely new constitution.</p></div>
</div>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-4 gap-3">
  <TremorCard label="Delegates" value="55 from 12 states" helper="All white men, mostly wealthy, college-educated lawyers and planters, averaging their early forties. Rhode Island refused to come." />
  <TremorCard label="Presiding" value="George Washington" helper="Elected chair unanimously. Benjamin Franklin, 81, was the calming elder statesman." />
  <TremorCard label="Architect" value="James Madison" helper="The 'Father of the Constitution,' working with Hamilton, Gouverneur Morris, and Dickinson." />
  <TremorCard label="Rules" value="Total secrecy" helper="Meetings were closed and nothing was published until the work was done. Jefferson and John Adams were abroad; Patrick Henry refused to attend." />
</div>

## 7.2 The big fight: representation

Should big states get more power than small ones? Three plans, one compromise. Then look at the chart: it shows exactly what the compromise did.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">Madison · favors big states</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">Virginia Plan</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Two houses, <strong>both</strong> based on population. Virginia and Pennsylvania would dominate.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">Paterson · favors small states</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">New Jersey Plan</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">One house with <strong>equal votes</strong> for every state, like the Articles. Delaware would count as much as Virginia.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-800 dark:text-accent-300 mb-1">Roger Sherman · the deal</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">Great (Connecticut) Compromise</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Two houses: a <strong>House of Representatives by population</strong> and a <strong>Senate with two members per state</strong>. Tax bills must start in the House.</p>
  </div>
</div>

<p class="not-prose text-sm font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-6 mb-0">Seats per state in the first Congress: the House (by population, set in Article I until the first census) vs the Senate (equal)</p>

<RechartsBar
  data={[
    { state: "NH", House: 3, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "MA", House: 8, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "RI", House: 1, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "CT", House: 5, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "NY", House: 6, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "NJ", House: 4, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "PA", House: 8, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "DE", House: 1, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "MD", House: 6, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "VA", House: 10, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "NC", House: 5, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "SC", House: 5, Senate: 2 },
    { state: "GA", House: 3, Senate: 2 }
  ]}
  xKey="state"
  bars={[
    { dataKey: "House", name: "House seats (by population)", color: "#b91c1c" },
    { dataKey: "Senate", name: "Senate seats (equal)", color: "#1d4ed8" }
  ]}
  showValues
  height={340}
/>

<Callout type="insight" title="Read the chart like a delegate">
  In the House, Virginia (10) outvotes Delaware (1) ten to one: the big states got what they wanted. In the Senate the blue bars are all the same height, so Delaware equals Virginia: the small states got what they wanted. That is the whole compromise in one picture.
</Callout>

## 7.3 The slavery compromises

Slavery could not be abolished at the convention: the Deep South would have walked out. So the delegates bargained over it instead.

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
  <p class="font-mono text-[11px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-3">The Three-Fifths Compromise, drawn out</p>
  <div class="flex items-end justify-center gap-3 text-5xl" aria-label="Five people shown; three are counted">
    <span>🧍🏿</span><span>🧍🏿</span><span>🧍🏿</span><span class="opacity-25">🧍🏿</span><span class="opacity-25">🧍🏿</span>
  </div>
  <p class="mt-3 text-center text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">For every <strong>five</strong> enslaved people, <strong>three</strong> were counted when dividing up House seats and direct taxes.</p>
  <div class="mt-4 grid sm:grid-cols-2 gap-3 text-sm">
    <div class="rounded-xl bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-800 p-3 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200"><strong>Virginia, 1790 census:</strong> 292,627 enslaved people counted as about <strong>175,576</strong> "persons" for House seats. More seats, and more electoral votes, for slaveholders.</div>
    <div class="rounded-xl bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/40 p-3 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200"><strong>The cruelty inside the math:</strong> enslaved people were counted to give <em>their enslavers</em> power, while being denied every right themselves.</div>
  </div>
</div>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="Compromise 1" value="Three-fifths" helper="Enslaved people count as 3/5 of a person for representation and direct taxes." />
  <TremorCard label="Compromise 2" value="Slave trade to 1808" helper="Congress could not ban the importation of enslaved people for 20 years. It banned it as soon as it could, in 1808." />
  <TremorCard label="Compromise 3" value="Fugitive slave clause" helper="People who escaped slavery into another state had to be returned." />
</div>

<Callout type="warning" title="The word that never appears">
  The Constitution never uses the word "slavery." It says "other persons" and "persons held to service or labour." The framers protected slavery while avoiding its name, which tells you they knew it clashed with the Declaration's ideals.
</Callout>

## 7.4 Trade and the presidency

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">⚖️ The Commerce Compromise</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-2">The <strong>North</strong> wanted the national government to regulate trade. The <strong>South</strong> feared taxes on its exports of tobacco and rice.</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">The deal: Congress regulates <strong>interstate and foreign commerce</strong> and may tax <strong>imports</strong> (tariffs), but may <strong>never tax exports</strong>.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-accent-900 dark:text-accent-100 mb-2">🦅 The presidency</h3>
    <ul class="space-y-1.5 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 list-disc list-inside">
      <li>One executive with a <strong>four-year term</strong> and no term limit (some delegates wanted a president for life)</li>
      <li>Real power: <strong>veto</strong> acts of Congress, command the military, make treaties and appointments with the Senate</li>
      <li>Chosen by the <strong>Electoral College</strong>: each state gets electors equal to its representatives plus senators, because many delegates feared that direct democracy could become mob rule</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
</div>

## 7.5 How the Constitution divides power

Six principles, then two diagrams. Know the principle names cold; the exam uses them as answer choices.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-3 gap-3">
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">👥 Popular sovereignty</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Power comes from the people: "We the People."</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">🗳️ Republicanism</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Citizens elect representatives to govern for them.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">🧩 Federalism</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Power divided between the national government and the states.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">🏛️ Separation of powers</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Legislative, executive, and judicial branches, each with its own job.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">🔁 Checks and balances</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Each branch can limit the other two, so none becomes a tyrant.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">📜 Limited government</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Government has only the powers the people grant it; the Bill of Rights marks what it may not do.</p></div>
</div>

### Checks and balances

Watch the checks draw themselves. Every red arrow is one branch's power over another.

<DrawnSVG duration={200} caption="Each red arrow is one branch checking another. Click the drawing or the button to replay.">
  <svg viewBox="0 0 640 440" class="w-full h-auto text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" role="img" aria-label="Checks and balances among the legislative, executive, and judicial branches">
    <rect x="220" y="20" width="200" height="72" rx="12" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2.5"/>
    <rect x="20" y="328" width="200" height="72" rx="12" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2.5"/>
    <rect x="420" y="328" width="200" height="72" rx="12" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2.5"/>

    <path d="M170.4 313.6 L172 326 L181.6 317.9" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5" stroke-linejoin="round"/>
    <path d="M378 94 L468 326" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5"/>
    <path d="M458.4 317.9 L468 326 L469.6 313.6" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5" stroke-linejoin="round"/>
    <path d="M503 326 L413 94" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5"/>
    <path d="M422.6 102.1 L413 94 L411.4 106.4" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5" stroke-linejoin="round"/>
    <path d="M222 350 L418 350" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5"/>
    <path d="M407 356 L418 350 L407 344" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5" stroke-linejoin="round"/>
    <path d="M418 378 L222 378" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5"/>
    <path d="M233 372 L222 378 L233 384" fill="none" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="2.5" stroke-linejoin="round"/>
    <text x="320" y="50" text-anchor="middle" font-size="15" font-weight="700" fill="#dc2626">LEGISLATIVE</text>
    <text x="320" y="72" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Congress makes the laws</text>
    <text x="120" y="358" text-anchor="middle" font-size="15" font-weight="700" fill="#dc2626">EXECUTIVE</text>
    <text x="120" y="380" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">President enforces them</text>
    <text x="520" y="358" text-anchor="middle" font-size="15" font-weight="700" fill="#dc2626">JUDICIAL</text>
    <text x="520" y="380" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Courts interpret them</text>
    <text x="170" y="210" text-anchor="end" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">vetoes bills</text>
    <text x="236" y="178" text-anchor="start" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">overrides vetoes (2/3)</text>
    <text x="236" y="195" text-anchor="start" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">impeaches + removes</text>
    <text x="236" y="212" text-anchor="start" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">confirms appointments</text>
    <text x="236" y="229" text-anchor="start" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">ratifies treaties (Senate)</text>
    <text x="432" y="262" text-anchor="end" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">confirms judges (Senate)</text>
    <text x="438" y="279" text-anchor="end" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">creates lower courts</text>
    <text x="468" y="200" text-anchor="start" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">judicial review:</text>
    <text x="468" y="217" text-anchor="start" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">can rule laws</text>
    <text x="468" y="234" text-anchor="start" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">unconstitutional*</text>
    <text x="320" y="342" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">nominates judges</text>
    <text x="320" y="396" text-anchor="middle" font-size="12" fill="currentColor">rules on its actions*</text>
    <text x="320" y="430" text-anchor="middle" font-size="11" fill="currentColor">* Judicial review was established by the Supreme Court in Marbury v. Madison (1803).</text>
  </svg>
</DrawnSVG>

### Federalism: who does what

<figure class="not-prose my-8 surface rounded-2xl shadow-soft p-5">
  <svg viewBox="0 0 640 360" class="w-full h-auto text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100" role="img" aria-label="Venn diagram of national, shared, and state powers">
    <circle cx="240" cy="180" r="160" fill="#b91c1c" fill-opacity="0.10" stroke="#dc2626" stroke-width="3"/>
    <circle cx="400" cy="180" r="160" fill="currentColor" fill-opacity="0.05" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="3"/>
    <text x="160" y="72" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="17" font-weight="700" fill="#dc2626">National</text>
    <text x="320" y="72" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="17" font-weight="700" fill="currentColor">Shared</text>
    <text x="480" y="72" text-anchor="middle" font-family="serif" font-size="17" font-weight="700" fill="currentColor">States</text>
    <text x="160" y="118" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Declare war</text>
    <text x="160" y="146" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Make treaties</text>
    <text x="160" y="174" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Coin money</text>
    <text x="160" y="202" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Regulate trade</text>
    <text x="160" y="218" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">between states</text>
    <text x="160" y="246" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Run post offices</text>
    <text x="320" y="132" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Collect taxes</text>
    <text x="320" y="160" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Borrow money</text>
    <text x="320" y="188" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Make + enforce laws</text>
    <text x="320" y="216" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Set up courts</text>
    <text x="480" y="118" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Run elections</text>
    <text x="480" y="146" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Set up schools</text>
    <text x="480" y="174" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Local governments</text>
    <text x="480" y="202" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Trade within</text>
    <text x="480" y="218" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">the state</text>
    <text x="480" y="246" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13" fill="currentColor">Marriage + licenses</text>
  </svg>
  <figcaption class="mt-3 text-sm text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 text-center">The 10th Amendment (1791) adds the rule for everything else: powers not given to the national government, nor denied to the states, are <strong>reserved to the states or the people</strong>.</figcaption>
</figure>

## 7.6 Ratification: closer than it looks

The framers knew the states might balk, so they required only **nine of thirteen** states to ratify, through special elected **conventions** rather than state legislatures. The chart shows the share of each convention that voted yes, in the order the states ratified. Everything under about 55 percent was a nail-biter.

<p class="not-prose text-sm font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-6 mb-0">Percent of each state's ratifying convention voting YES, in order of ratification (December 1787 to May 1790)</p>

<RechartsBar
  data={[
    { state: "DE", yes: 100 },
    { state: "PA", yes: 67 },
    { state: "NJ", yes: 100 },
    { state: "GA", yes: 100 },
    { state: "CT", yes: 76 },
    { state: "MA", yes: 53 },
    { state: "MD", yes: 85 },
    { state: "SC", yes: 67 },
    { state: "NH", yes: 55 },
    { state: "VA", yes: 53 },
    { state: "NY", yes: 53 },
    { state: "NC", yes: 72 },
    { state: "RI", yes: 52 }
  ]}
  xKey="state"
  bars={[{ dataKey: "yes", name: "Percent voting yes", color: "#b91c1c" }]}
  showValues
  valueSuffix="%"
  height={320}
/>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="The 9th state" value="New Hampshire" helper="June 21, 1788, by 57 to 47. The Constitution is officially ratified." />
  <TremorCard label="The ones that mattered" value="Virginia + New York" helper="The biggest states said yes by 89-79 and 30-27. Without them the new nation would have been split in pieces." />
  <TremorCard label="The holdouts" value="NC 1789 · RI 1790" helper="Both first refused. Rhode Island, which never came to Philadelphia, finally ratified by 34 to 32." />
</div>

<Collapsible question="See every state's vote count (the table view of the chart)">
  Delaware 30-0 (Dec 7, 1787) · Pennsylvania 46-23 · New Jersey 38-0 · Georgia 26-0 (Jan 2, 1788) · Connecticut 128-40 · Massachusetts 187-168 · Maryland 63-11 · South Carolina 149-73 · New Hampshire 57-47 (June 21, 1788, the ninth state) · Virginia 89-79 · New York 30-27 · North Carolina 194-77 (Nov 21, 1789) · Rhode Island 34-32 (May 29, 1790).
</Collapsible>

## 7.7 Federalists vs Anti-Federalists

The ratification fight created the first national political sides. Learn the comparison as a grid.

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 overflow-hidden text-sm">
  <div class="grid grid-cols-[7rem_1fr_1fr] sm:grid-cols-[9rem_1fr_1fr]">
    <div class="px-3 py-2 bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800"></div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 bg-accent-700 text-white font-bold">Federalists</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 bg-ink-900 dark:bg-ink-200 text-white dark:text-ink-900 font-bold">Anti-Federalists</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Position</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Ratify the Constitution</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Reject it, or amend the Articles instead</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Leaders</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Washington, Franklin, Madison, Hamilton, Jay</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">George Mason, Patrick Henry, George Clinton, John Hancock</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Supporters</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Merchants and cities along the Atlantic coast</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Small farmers and settlers on the western frontier</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Argument</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">A stronger central government is needed to keep order and preserve the Union</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">It will destroy the work of the Revolution, crush states' rights, and has no bill of rights</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Strengths</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Famous leaders, well organized, a concrete plan</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Popular distrust of distant power, rooted in the colonial experience</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Weaknesses</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">The Constitution was new, untried, and had no bill of rights</div>
    <div class="px-3 py-2 border-t border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Poorly organized and slow to answer the Federalists</div>
  </div>
</div>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="The Federalist Papers" value="85 essays, 1787-88" helper="Written for New York newspapers by Hamilton, Madison, and Jay under the pen name 'Publius.' The best defense of the Constitution ever written." />
  <TremorCard label="The deal that won" value="Promise a bill of rights" helper="Federalists answered the Anti-Federalists' strongest objection by promising amendments. That promise carried Massachusetts, Virginia, and New York." />
</div>

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="James Madison, Federalist No. 51, 1788">
  If men were angels, no government would be necessary.
</SpeechBubble>

<SpeechBubble variant="shout" speaker="Patrick Henry, Virginia ratifying convention, June 1788">
  Who authorized them to speak the language of, We, the people, instead of We, the states?
</SpeechBubble>

<Callout type="tip" title="Madison's big idea (Federalist No. 10)">
  Anti-Federalists said a republic could only work in a small area. Madison flipped it: in a <strong>large republic</strong> there are so many competing groups (factions) that no single one can become a tyrannical majority. Extend the sphere and liberty gets <em>safer</em>. If a question asks how Federalists answered fears of a large republic, this is it.
</Callout>

## 7.8 The Bill of Rights answers the Revolution

The first Congress, led by **Madison**, drafted the amendments in 1789, and the states ratified ten of them in **1791**. Many of them are direct answers to grievances from 1763 to 1775. Drag each grievance onto the amendment that answered it.

<DragMatch
  title="Match each grievance to the amendment that answered it"
  pairs={[
    { left: "Quartering Acts: soldiers lodged in colonists' buildings", right: "3rd: no soldiers quartered in a home without the owner's consent" },
    { left: "Writs of assistance: general warrants to search anywhere", right: "4th: no unreasonable searches; warrants must name the place" },
    { left: "Admiralty courts and trials moved to Britain", right: "6th: a speedy, public trial by a local jury" },
    { left: "British troops march to seize militia arms at Concord", right: "2nd: a well-regulated militia and the right to bear arms" },
    { left: "Printers prosecuted for criticizing royal officials", right: "1st: freedom of speech, press, religion, assembly, petition" },
    { left: "Property seized and people punished without fair process", right: "5th: no one deprived of life, liberty, or property without due process" },
    { left: "Anti-Federalist fear: unlisted rights will be ignored", right: "9th: listing some rights does not deny others kept by the people" },
    { left: "Anti-Federalist fear: the national government will swallow the states", right: "10th: powers not delegated are reserved to the states or the people" }
  ]}
/>

# Section 8 · Washington Builds a Government

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl overflow-hidden bg-gradient-to-br from-ink-950 via-accent-950 to-ink-900 px-6 py-10 text-center">
  <p class="font-mono text-white/60 text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.25em] mb-3">AMSCO Ch. 6 · Topic 3.10</p>
  <h2 class="font-serif text-3xl md:text-5xl font-bold text-white mb-2">Shaping a New Republic</h2>
  <p class="text-white/80 max-w-xl mx-auto">The Constitution was a blueprint. Washington, Hamilton, and Jefferson had to decide what it actually meant, and they did not agree.</p>
</div>

## 8.1 Precedents: everything Washington did was a first

Washington was elected **unanimously** by the Electoral College and took the oath on **April 30, 1789**, in New York City, the temporary capital. He knew every choice would become a rule for the presidents after him.

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-4 gap-3">
  <TremorCard label="Precedent" value="The cabinet" helper="The Constitution never mentions one. Washington met regularly with his department heads for advice, and every president since has too." />
  <TremorCard label="Precedent" value="Two terms" helper="He retired after eight years. Presidents followed that example until FDR; the 22nd Amendment (1951) made it law." />
  <TremorCard label="Precedent" value="Federal courts" helper="The Judiciary Act of 1789: a Supreme Court of six justices (one chief), plus 13 district courts and 3 circuit courts." />
  <TremorCard label="Precedent" value="Neutrality" helper="Stay out of Europe's wars (1793). The foundation of American foreign policy for a century." />
</div>

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-4 gap-3">
  <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4 text-center"><div class="text-3xl">🌐</div><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-500 mt-1">Secretary of State</p><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Thomas Jefferson</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4 text-center"><div class="text-3xl">💰</div><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-500 mt-1">Secretary of the Treasury</p><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Alexander Hamilton</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4 text-center"><div class="text-3xl">⚔️</div><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-500 mt-1">Secretary of War</p><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Henry Knox</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4 text-center"><div class="text-3xl">⚖️</div><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest text-ink-500 mt-1">Attorney General</p><p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Edmund Randolph</p></div>
</div>

The two red cards are the story of the 1790s: the two most brilliant members of the cabinet wanted opposite countries.

## 8.2 Hamilton's financial plan

Hamilton inherited a bankrupt nation. His plan rested on three pillars, all aimed at making the United States **creditworthy** and tying wealthy Americans' interests to the new national government.

<div class="not-prose my-8">
  <div class="rounded-t-2xl bg-accent-700 text-white text-center py-3 px-4"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest opacity-80">Goal</p><p class="font-serif text-xl font-bold">Strong national credit and a growing economy</p></div>
  <div class="grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-3 mt-3">
    <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Pillar 1</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Pay the debts in full</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Pay the national debt at <strong>face value</strong> and have the federal government <strong>assume the states' war debts</strong>. Critics: speculators who had bought old IOUs cheaply from veterans would get rich.</p></div>
    <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Pillar 2</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Tariffs</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Taxes on imports to raise revenue and <strong>protect "infant" American industries</strong> from British competition. Congress passed them, but lower than Hamilton wanted.</p></div>
    <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Pillar 3</p><p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">A national bank</p><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">The <strong>Bank of the United States</strong> (1791): privately owned, federally chartered, holding government funds and issuing notes for a stable currency.</p></div>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-b-2xl border-2 border-t-0 border-ink-300 dark:border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-900 text-center py-3 px-4 mt-3 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Plus an <strong>excise tax on whiskey</strong> to make up for the lower tariffs. Remember it: it causes a rebellion in 1794.</div>
</div>

<Callout type="note" title="The deal behind the debt: the Compromise of 1790">
  Southern states, many of which had already paid off their debts, hated assumption. So Hamilton made a deal with Jefferson and Madison: they would support assumption, and in return the permanent national capital would be built in the <strong>South, on the Potomac River</strong>. That capital became Washington, D.C.
</Callout>

## 8.3 Loose vs strict: the fight over the bank

Nothing in the Constitution says Congress may charter a bank. Jefferson said that settled it. Hamilton pointed to the last clause of Congress's list of powers. Hover the highlights to see the two readings.

<DocAnnotate
  title="Article I, Section 8 of the Constitution (excerpt)"
  text="The Congress shall have Power To lay and collect Taxes, Duties, Imposts and Excises, to pay the Debts and provide for the common Defence and general Welfare of the United States; To borrow Money on the credit of the United States; To regulate Commerce with foreign Nations, and among the several States... To make all Laws which shall be necessary and proper for carrying into Execution the foregoing Powers, and all other Powers vested by this Constitution in the Government of the United States, or in any Department or Officer thereof."
  annotations={[
    { start: 30, end: 54, label: "Enumerated power: taxing", note: "Hamilton: a bank helps the government collect and hold its taxes." },
    { start: 179, end: 194, label: "Enumerated power: borrowing", note: "Hamilton: a bank is where a government borrows. None of these listed powers mentions a bank, which is Jefferson's whole point." },
    { start: 231, end: 251, label: "Enumerated power: commerce", note: "Hamilton: a stable national currency makes trade between the states work." },
    { start: 338, end: 358, label: "The elastic clause", note: "LOOSE construction (Hamilton): 'necessary' means useful or appropriate, so Congress has implied powers. STRICT construction (Jefferson): 'necessary' means truly indispensable, and a bank is not." },
    { start: 359, end: 407, label: "The link that matters", note: "Implied powers must connect to a listed power. Hamilton tied the bank to taxing, borrowing, and commerce. Washington agreed and signed the bank bill in 1791." }
  ]}
/>

<Callout type="warning" title="A common mistake in notes">
  Hamilton did not <em>create</em> the "necessary and proper" clause; the framers wrote it into the Constitution in 1787. Hamilton <strong>interpreted</strong> it broadly to justify implied powers. Say "Hamilton used the necessary and proper clause" and you are safe.
</Callout>

## 8.4 Two visions of America

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-teal-700 bg-teal-50 dark:bg-teal-950/30 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-teal-800 dark:text-teal-300 mb-1">Federalist</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-2xl font-bold text-teal-900 dark:text-teal-100 mb-3">Alexander Hamilton</h3>
    <ul class="space-y-1.5 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">
      <li>🏛️ <strong>Strong</strong> federal government</li>
      <li>📖 <strong>Loose</strong> construction of the Constitution</li>
      <li>🏭 Future in <strong>commerce and manufacturing</strong></li>
      <li>🏦 <strong>For</strong> the national bank and high tariffs</li>
      <li>🇬🇧 Friendly to <strong>Britain</strong>, America's biggest trading partner</li>
      <li>🎩 Trusted the educated, wealthy elite to govern</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-800 dark:text-accent-300 mb-1">Democratic-Republican</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-2xl font-bold text-accent-900 dark:text-accent-100 mb-3">Thomas Jefferson</h3>
    <ul class="space-y-1.5 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">
      <li>🏡 <strong>Limited</strong> federal government, stronger states</li>
      <li>📖 <strong>Strict</strong> construction of the Constitution</li>
      <li>🌾 Future in <strong>independent farmers</strong> and agriculture</li>
      <li>🚫 <strong>Against</strong> the national bank and high tariffs</li>
      <li>⚜️ Sympathetic to <strong>revolutionary France</strong></li>
      <li>👨‍🌾 Trusted ordinary (white, male) citizens</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
</div>

## 8.5 Whiskey vs Shays: the same test, two governments

Two rebellions of indebted western farmers, eight years apart. The difference in how each government responded is one of the best comparisons in the unit.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-400 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">1786-1787 · under the Articles</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">Shays' Rebellion</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-3">Massachusetts farmers vs high <strong>state</strong> taxes and debtors' courts.</p>
    <div class="h-2 rounded-full bg-ink-200 dark:bg-ink-700 overflow-hidden" aria-hidden="true"><div class="h-2 w-[12%] bg-ink-500"></div></div>
    <p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-2"><strong>National response: almost none.</strong> Congress could not fund troops; a privately paid state militia ended it. Proof the Articles were too weak.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-800 dark:text-accent-300 mb-1">1794 · under the Constitution</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">Whiskey Rebellion</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 mb-3">Western Pennsylvania farmers vs the <strong>federal</strong> excise tax on whiskey; they attacked tax collectors.</p>
    <div class="h-2 rounded-full bg-ink-200 dark:bg-ink-700 overflow-hidden" aria-hidden="true"><div class="h-2 w-full bg-accent-700"></div></div>
    <p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-2"><strong>National response: overwhelming.</strong> Washington federalized about 15,000 militiamen (led by Hamilton) and the revolt collapsed with almost no bloodshed. Westerners resented it, and Jefferson gained popularity as the farmer's champion.</p>
  </div>
</div>

## 8.6 Caught between Britain and France

In 1793 revolutionary France went to war with Britain, and the question of whom to support split Americans for the next seven years. Watch Washington try to stay out.

<HistoryAnimation

    {
