## 8.7 The West and Native nations

North of the Ohio River, a **Western Confederacy** of Native nations (Shawnee, Delaware, Miami, and others, led by the Miami war chief **Little Turtle**) defeated American forces twice around 1790 and 1791, with arms supplied by the British from their forts. Then:

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="1794" value="Battle of Fallen Timbers" helper="General Anthony Wayne's army defeats the Confederacy in northwestern Ohio." />
  <TremorCard label="1795" value="Treaty of Greenville" helper="The defeated nations give up most of the Ohio country, opening it to settlement." />
  <TremorCard label="The pattern" value="Expansion at their expense" helper="Jay's Treaty removed the British forts the same year. American expansion kept coming at Native peoples' cost." />
</div>

## 8.8 Washington's Farewell Address

In 1796, with help from Hamilton, Washington published a farewell message in the newspapers. It carried enormous weight because of who wrote it.

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="George Washington, Farewell Address, 1796">
  It is our true policy to steer clear of permanent alliances with any portion of the foreign world.
</SpeechBubble>

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="George Washington, Farewell Address, 1796">
  Let me now... warn you in the most solemn manner against the baneful effects of the spirit of party generally.
</SpeechBubble>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-4 gap-3">
  <TremorCard label="Warning 1" value="Europe's affairs" helper="Stay out of European conflicts." />
  <TremorCard label="Warning 2" value="Permanent alliances" helper="Trade with everyone, but make no permanent political alliances. Heeded for over a century." />
  <TremorCard label="Warning 3" value="Political parties" helper="Parties breed faction and division. Already too late: two parties were forming." />
  <TremorCard label="Warning 4" value="Sectionalism" helper="Do not let North, South, and West think of themselves as rivals. National unity first." />
</div>

<Callout type="warning" title="Do not oversimplify the Farewell Address">
  Washington did not say "never deal with foreign countries." He wanted <strong>commerce with everyone</strong> and <strong>permanent political alliances with no one</strong>. A multiple-choice option saying he urged total isolation is a trap.
</Callout>

## 8.9 The first party system

Washington hoped the nation could avoid parties. Hamilton's program and the French Revolution made that impossible. Follow the two columns down: the ratification fight of 1787-88 turned into the first parties of the 1790s. Watch for the starred name.

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
  <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 dark:text-ink-400 mb-2">1787-1788 · The ratification fight</p>
  <div class="grid sm:grid-cols-2 gap-3">
    <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-ink-700 dark:border-ink-300 p-4">
      <p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Federalists</p>
      <p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mb-2">For the Constitution and a strong national government</p>
      <div class="flex flex-wrap gap-1.5"><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">Hamilton</span><span class="rounded-md bg-amber-100 dark:bg-amber-900/50 text-amber-900 dark:text-amber-100 border border-amber-500 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-bold">★ Madison</span><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">Jay</span><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">Washington</span><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">Franklin</span></div>
    </div>
    <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-dashed border-ink-400 dark:border-ink-500 p-4">
      <p class="font-serif font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">Anti-Federalists</p>
      <p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mb-2">Against it, above all without a bill of rights</p>
      <div class="flex flex-wrap gap-1.5"><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">George Mason</span><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">Patrick Henry</span><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">George Clinton</span></div>
    </div>
  </div>
  <div class="grid grid-cols-2 gap-3 my-2 text-center" aria-hidden="true">
    <span class="text-2xl font-bold text-teal-700 dark:text-teal-400">↓</span>
    <span class="text-2xl font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">↓</span>
  </div>
  <p class="text-center text-xs font-semibold text-amber-800 dark:text-amber-300 -mt-1 mb-3">★ Madison crosses over: pro-Constitution in 1788, anti-Hamilton by 1792</p>
  <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 dark:text-ink-400 mb-2">1790s · The first political parties</p>
  <div class="grid sm:grid-cols-2 gap-3">
    <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-teal-700 bg-teal-50 dark:bg-teal-950/30 p-4">
      <p class="font-serif font-bold text-teal-900 dark:text-teal-100">Federalist Party</p>
      <div class="flex flex-wrap gap-1.5 my-2"><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">Hamilton</span><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">John Adams</span><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">John Jay</span></div>
      <p class="text-xs text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Loose construction · national bank · commerce and manufacturing · pro-British · northern merchants</p>
    </div>
    <div class="rounded-xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-4">
      <p class="font-serif font-bold text-accent-900 dark:text-accent-100">Democratic-Republican Party</p>
      <div class="flex flex-wrap gap-1.5 my-2"><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">Jefferson</span><span class="rounded-md bg-amber-100 dark:bg-amber-900/50 text-amber-900 dark:text-amber-100 border border-amber-500 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-bold">★ Madison</span><span class="rounded-md bg-ink-100 dark:bg-ink-800 text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 px-2 py-0.5 text-xs font-semibold">many former Anti-Federalists</span></div>
      <p class="text-xs text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Strict construction · no bank · farmers and agriculture · pro-French · the South and West</p>
    </div>
  </div>
</div>

<Callout type="insight" title="Madison's switch">
  In 1787-88 Madison was a leading <strong>Federalist</strong> (in the ratification sense): he co-wrote the Federalist Papers. By the 1790s he co-founded the <strong>Democratic-Republicans</strong> with Jefferson to oppose Hamilton. He had not changed his mind about the Constitution; he opposed how Hamilton was <em>using</em> it. The Democratic-Republicans absorbed many former Anti-Federalists.
</Callout>

| | Federalists | Democratic-Republicans |
|---|---|---|
| Leaders | Hamilton, John Adams | Jefferson, Madison |
| Constitution | Loose construction | Strict construction |
| Central government | Strong | Limited; states' rights |
| Economy | Business, manufacturing, national bank, high tariffs | Agriculture; against the bank and high tariffs |
| Foreign policy | Pro-British | Pro-French |
| Military | Large peacetime army and navy | Small peacetime army and navy |
| Base | Northern merchants, business owners, large landowners | Small farmers, skilled workers, planters; the South and West |

## Section 8 self-quiz

<Quiz
  title="Washington's presidency"
  questions={[
    {
      q: "Which of these was a precedent set by George Washington rather than a requirement of the Constitution?",
      choices: [
        "The president's power to veto acts of Congress",
        "The Senate's role in confirming appointments",
        "Meeting regularly with a cabinet of department heads",
        "The four-year length of each presidential term"
      ],
      answer: 2,
      explain: "The Constitution never mentions a cabinet. The veto, Senate confirmation, and four-year term are all written in the document."
    },
    {
      q: "Hamilton's plan to have the federal government assume state war debts was opposed most strongly by",
      choices: [
        "southern states that had already paid off much of their debt",
        "northern merchants who held large amounts of government bonds",
        "British bankers who feared losing their American loans",
        "Federalists in Congress who wanted lower federal spending"
      ],
      answer: 0,
      explain: "States like Virginia saw no reason to help pay other states' debts. The Compromise of 1790 traded assumption for a southern capital on the Potomac."
    },
    {
      q: "Hamilton argued that the Bank of the United States was constitutional because",
      choices: [
        "the Constitution explicitly authorizes Congress to charter banks",
        "the states had approved the bank in their ratifying conventions",
        "the Supreme Court had already ruled in favor of a national bank",
        "the necessary and proper clause gave Congress implied powers"
      ],
      answer: 3,
      explain: "Loose construction: a bank was a reasonable means of carrying out the listed powers to tax, borrow, and regulate commerce. Jefferson's strict reading said no."
    },
    {
      q: "Washington's response to the Whiskey Rebellion (1794) demonstrated that",
      choices: [
        "the federal government could not collect taxes in the West",
        "the new federal government could enforce its laws",
        "western farmers controlled the Democratic-Republican Party",
        "states could nullify federal taxes they considered unfair"
      ],
      answer: 1,
      explain: "About 15,000 militiamen made the rebellion collapse with almost no bloodshed. Compare Shays' Rebellion, when the Articles government could do nothing."
    },
    {
      q: "Washington issued the Proclamation of Neutrality (1793) mainly because he believed",
      choices: [
        "the United States was too weak to risk involvement in a European war",
        "the French alliance of 1778 required America to stay neutral",
        "Britain would reward American neutrality with the western forts",
        "the Senate had refused to approve a declaration of war on Britain"
      ],
      answer: 0,
      explain: "The young nation had no real army or navy to spare. Jefferson disagreed and later resigned from the cabinet."
    },
    {
      q: "Jay's Treaty (1794) was unpopular with many Americans mainly because it",
      choices: [
        "gave Spain control of the port of New Orleans",
        "required the United States to fight for Britain against France",
        "did nothing to stop British impressment of American sailors",
        "returned the Ohio Valley to Britain's Native allies"
      ],
      answer: 2,
      explain: "Britain agreed to leave its western forts, but the treaty ignored ship seizures and impressment. It angered France's friends but kept the peace."
    },
    {
      q: "Pinckney's Treaty (1795) was especially popular with western farmers because it",
      choices: [
        "removed the British army from the western forts",
        "opened the Mississippi River and New Orleans to American trade",
        "ended the excise tax on whiskey distilled in the West",
        "guaranteed them free land north of the Ohio River"
      ],
      answer: 1,
      explain: "Western crops floated down the Mississippi to market. The right of deposit at New Orleans let them ship without Spanish duties."
    },
    {
      q: "In his Farewell Address, Washington warned Americans against",
      choices: [
        "trading with European nations of any kind",
        "creating a national bank and a standing army",
        "admitting new states from the western territories",
        "permanent foreign alliances and political parties"
      ],
      answer: 3,
      explain: "He urged commerce with all nations but permanent alliances with none, and warned against the 'spirit of party' and sectionalism."
    }
  ]}
/>

# Section 9 · Adams and the Revolution of 1800

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl overflow-hidden bg-gradient-to-br from-ink-950 via-accent-950 to-ink-900 px-6 py-10 text-center">
  <p class="font-mono text-white/60 text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.25em] mb-3">AMSCO Ch. 6 · Topic 3.10</p>
  <h2 class="font-serif text-3xl md:text-5xl font-bold text-white mb-2">A Crisis, Then a Peaceful Handoff</h2>
  <p class="text-white/80 max-w-xl mx-auto">A war scare, laws that jailed critics, states claiming they could cancel federal law, and a tied election. The republic survived all of it.</p>
</div>

## 9.1 The awkward election of 1796

Federalist **John Adams** beat Democratic-Republican **Thomas Jefferson** by just three electoral votes (71 to 68). Under the original Constitution, the runner-up became vice president, so the president and vice president came from **rival parties**. The 12th Amendment (1804) later had electors vote for president and vice president separately.

## 9.2 The XYZ Affair and the Quasi-War

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-4 gap-3">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><div class="text-3xl mb-1">⚓</div><p class="font-bold text-sm text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">1. France strikes</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Angry about Jay's Treaty, French warships and privateers seize American merchant ships.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><div class="text-3xl mb-1">💰</div><p class="font-bold text-sm text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">2. X, Y, and Z</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">Adams sends diplomats to Paris. Three French agents, later identified only as X, Y, and Z, demand a bribe before talks can even begin. The Americans refuse.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-4"><div class="text-3xl mb-1">📰</div><p class="font-bold text-sm text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">3. Outrage</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-200 mt-1">"Millions for defense, but not one cent for tribute!" Federalists (Hamilton's wing) want war and a chance to grab French and Spanish lands.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-4"><div class="text-3xl mb-1">🕊️</div><p class="font-bold text-sm text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">4. Adams holds back</p><p class="text-xs text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mt-1">He builds up the navy and fights an undeclared naval <strong>Quasi-War</strong> (1798-1800), but refuses a full war and sends new envoys. Peace comes in 1800.</p></div>
</div>

## 9.3 The Alien and Sedition Acts, 1798

War fever gave the Federalists big majorities in Congress in 1798, and they used them against their political opponents. Most immigrants voted Democratic-Republican, and Democratic-Republican newspapers attacked Adams daily.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">Law 1</p><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Naturalization Act</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Raised the residency requirement for citizenship from <strong>5 to 14 years</strong>. Fewer new (Democratic-Republican) voters.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-500 mb-1">Law 2</p><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Alien Acts</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Let the president <strong>deport</strong> any alien he considered dangerous, and jail enemy aliens in wartime.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-800 dark:text-accent-300 mb-1">Law 3 · the worst</p><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Sedition Act</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Made it a crime to publish false, scandalous, or malicious criticism of the government, Congress, or the president. Democratic-Republican editors, and even Vermont congressman <strong>Matthew Lyon</strong>, went to jail.</p></div>
</div>

## 9.4 The Kentucky and Virginia Resolutions

With no Supreme Court precedent yet for striking down laws (judicial review arrives in 1803), Jefferson and Madison turned to the states. They wrote resolutions **secretly**; Jefferson's passed in Kentucky, Madison's in Virginia.

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="Kentucky Resolutions, drafted secretly by Vice President Jefferson, 1798">
  Whensoever the general government assumes undelegated powers, its acts are unauthoritative, void, and of no force.
</SpeechBubble>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="The theory" value="The compact" helper="The states created the national government by agreement (a compact), so the states can judge when it breaks the deal." />
  <TremorCard label="The remedy" value="Nullification" helper="A state may declare an unconstitutional federal law void within its borders. No other state joined them in 1798-99." />
  <TremorCard label="The legacy" value="States' rights" helper="The same argument returns in the Nullification Crisis of the 1830s and in the road to the Civil War." />
</div>

## 9.5 You are John Adams

It is spring 1798. Play the crisis, then read what Adams actually did.

<BranchingScenario
  title="Philadelphia, spring 1798"
  context="You are President John Adams. The XYZ dispatches are public: French agents demanded a bribe for their foreign minister, Talleyrand, plus a large loan to France, before they would even talk to your diplomats. French ships are seizing American merchant vessels. Your own Federalist Party, with Alexander Hamilton pulling strings behind the scenes, is roaring for war. The Democratic-Republicans, friends of France, call you a warmonger and worse."
  historicalAnswer="Adams took the middle road. He built up the navy (creating the Navy Department in 1798) and fought an undeclared naval Quasi-War, but never asked Congress to declare war, and in 1799 he sent new envoys to France over his own party's furious objections. The Convention of 1800 ended the conflict and the 1778 alliance. Adams also signed the Alien and Sedition Acts, which backfired badly: they made Jefferson's party look like the defender of liberty, split the Federalists, and helped cost Adams the Election of 1800. Years later Adams said he wanted to be remembered for making peace with France in 1800."
  tree={{
    start: "opening",
    nodes: {
      opening: {
        scenario: "Congress and the newspapers are waiting for your answer to the insult. What do you do?",
        choices: [
          { text: "Ask Congress to declare war on France", next: "war" },
          { text: "Build up the navy, fight at sea if attacked, and keep negotiating", next: "middle" },
          { text: "Pay the bribe quietly to keep the peace", next: "bribe" }
        ]
      },
      war: {
        ending: "Congress declares war. Hamilton gets command of a large new army and dreams of seizing Spanish Florida and Louisiana. Taxes soar, the tiny navy is stretched thin, and the Democratic-Republicans brand the Federalists a party of generals. Your presidency becomes a war presidency you never wanted.",
        historicalNote: "Adams never asked for a declaration of war. He distrusted Hamilton's ambitions and doubted the country was ready."
      },
      bribe: {
        ending: "Word leaks, as it always does. 'Millions for defense, but not one cent for tribute!' becomes a chant against YOU. Your own party turns on you, France learns that bullying works, and ship seizures continue.",
        historicalNote: "Paying tribute was politically impossible. The public reaction to the XYZ dispatches was pure fury."
      },
      middle: {
        scenario: "Your navy is growing and the Quasi-War is underway at sea. Now Federalists in Congress send you four bills: tougher citizenship rules, powers to deport dangerous aliens, and a Sedition Act making it a crime to publish false or malicious criticism of the government. Newspapers attack you daily. Do you sign?",
        choices: [
          { text: "Sign them: the nation is at war and critics endanger it", next: "sign" },
          { text: "Veto them: they violate the First Amendment", next: "veto" }
        ]
      },
      sign: {
        ending: "Editors and even a congressman go to jail. Jefferson and Madison answer with the Kentucky and Virginia Resolutions, and Democratic-Republicans pose as the defenders of free speech. When you later make peace with France, Hamilton's wing of your own party turns on you. In 1800 the Federalists lose the presidency and Congress.",
        historicalNote: "This is what happened. The Alien and Sedition Acts are the most damaging mistake of Adams's presidency."
      },
      veto: {
        ending: "Hamilton's Federalists are furious and call you weak. But Jefferson loses his best issue, and when you make peace with France, you stand on stronger ground. Maybe the Federalists last longer. Maybe not: the party's base in the Northeast was shrinking anyway.",
        historicalNote: "A counterfactual. Adams signed the acts; historians still debate how much they, versus the war taxes and the Federalist split, cost him in 1800."
      }
    }
  }}
/>

## 9.6 The Election of 1800

Democratic-Republican electors each cast two votes, one for **Jefferson** and one for his running mate **Aaron Burr**, and nobody remembered to throw away a vote for Burr. The result was a tie.

<p class="not-prose text-sm font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-6 mb-0">Electoral votes in 1800, colored by party (each elector voted for two people)</p>

<RechartsBar
  data={[
    { candidate: "Jefferson", dr: 73, fed: 0 },
    { candidate: "Burr", dr: 73, fed: 0 },
    { candidate: "Adams", dr: 0, fed: 65 },
    { candidate: "C. C. Pinckney", dr: 0, fed: 64 },
    { candidate: "Jay", dr: 0, fed: 1 }
  ]}
  xKey="candidate"
  bars={[
    { dataKey: "dr", name: "Democratic-Republican", color: "#b91c1c" },
    { dataKey: "fed", name: "Federalist", color: "#0d9488" }
  ]}
  stacked
  showValues
  height={320}
/>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-4 gap-3">
  <TremorCard label="The tie" value="73 to 73" helper="Jefferson and Burr tied, so the House of Representatives had to choose." />
  <TremorCard label="The House" value="36 ballots" helper="The lame-duck, Federalist-controlled House deadlocked for days in February 1801." />
  <TremorCard label="The kingmaker" value="Hamilton" helper="He disliked Jefferson but considered Burr dangerous and unprincipled, and urged Federalists to let Jefferson win." />
  <TremorCard label="The fix" value="12th Amendment" helper="Ratified in 1804: separate electoral ballots for president and vice president." />
</div>

<SpeechBubble variant="say" speaker="Thomas Jefferson, First Inaugural Address, March 4, 1801">
  We are all Republicans, we are all Federalists.
</SpeechBubble>

<Callout type="insight" title="Why 1800 was a 'revolution'">
  In 1801 power passed from one party to its bitter rival <strong>without violence</strong>. The defeated Federalists simply left office. In the world of 1800, that almost never happened, and it proved the constitutional system could survive fierce partisanship. Jefferson called it the <strong>Revolution of 1800</strong>. The Federalists lost the presidency and both houses of Congress, and they never won the presidency again.
</Callout>

# Section 10 · An American Identity on the Move

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl overflow-hidden bg-gradient-to-br from-ink-950 via-accent-950 to-ink-900 px-6 py-10 text-center">
  <p class="font-mono text-white/60 text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.25em] mb-3">AMSCO Ch. 6 · Topics 3.11 + 3.12</p>
  <h2 class="font-serif text-3xl md:text-5xl font-bold text-white mb-2">A Nation Finds Its Face, and Heads West</h2>
  <p class="text-white/80 max-w-xl mx-auto">By 1800 Americans were painting their own heroes, planning their own capital, and pouring over the mountains. The West brought opportunity, conflict, and a new future for slavery.</p>
</div>

## 10.1 A national culture

Americans still read British books and copied British fashions, but a distinctly **national** culture started to form around the Revolution's heroes and ideals.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><div class="text-3xl mb-2">🖼️</div><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Charles Willson Peale</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Painted Washington and other Revolutionary leaders from life, and opened one of the first American museums in Philadelphia. Art as a gallery of national heroes.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><div class="text-3xl mb-2">🎨</div><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Gilbert Stuart</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">The great portraitist of Washington. His unfinished 1796 portrait is the face on the one-dollar bill: the image of the founder most Americans still carry around.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><div class="text-3xl mb-2">📐</div><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Pierre Charles L'Enfant</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">The French-born engineer who designed the plan for Washington, D.C. (1791): grand avenues radiating from the Capitol and the President's House. A capital built to look like a republic.</p></div>
</div>

<Callout type="insight" title="Change and continuity, again">
  <strong>Change:</strong> national heroes, a planned national capital, and religious freedom as a national value. <strong>Continuity:</strong> British language, law, literature, and style stayed dominant. American identity grew <em>on top of</em> British culture rather than replacing it.
</Callout>

## 10.2 Moving west

The population grew fast, and settlers poured over the Appalachians. Three new states joined the Union in five years. Red dots are settlements and new states; black dots are conflicts.

<MapView
  title="The early republic moves west, 1775 to 1796"
  center={[39.9, -79.2]}
  zoom={5}
  height="500px"
  showLabels
  markers={[
    { lat: 36.60, lng: -83.67, label: "Cumberland Gap (Wilderness Road, 1775)", year: "1775", popup: "Daniel Boone blazes the Wilderness Road through this mountain pass into Kentucky. Tens of thousands of settlers follow.", color: "#b91c1c", labelDir: "right" },
    { lat: 37.90, lng: -84.27, label: "Kentucky: state 15 (1792)", year: "1792", popup: "Boone's Boonesborough was an early settlement. Kentucky became the first state west of the Appalachians.", color: "#b91c1c", labelDir: "left" },
    { lat: 35.96, lng: -83.92, label: "Tennessee: state 16 (1796)", year: "1796", popup: "Admitted smoothly under the Constitution's process for new states.", color: "#b91c1c", labelDir: "left" },
    { lat: 44.26, lng: -72.58, label: "Vermont: state 14 (1791)", year: "1791", popup: "The first new state admitted under the Constitution.", color: "#b91c1c", labelDir: "right" },
    { lat: 39.42, lng: -81.45, label: "Marietta, Ohio (1788)", year: "1788", popup: "The first permanent American settlement in the Northwest Territory, organized under the Northwest Ordinance.", color: "#b91c1c", labelDir: "left" },
    { lat: 40.17, lng: -80.25, label: "Whiskey Rebellion (1794)", year: "1794", popup: "Western Pennsylvania farmers resist the federal whiskey tax. Washington's militia ends it.", color: "#18181b", labelDir: "right" },
    { lat: 41.54, lng: -83.70, label: "Fallen Timbers (1794)", year: "1794", popup: "Anthony Wayne defeats the Western Confederacy.", color: "#18181b", labelDir: "left" },
    { lat: 40.10, lng: -84.63, label: "Treaty of Greenville (1795)", year: "1795", popup: "Native nations give up most of the Ohio country.", color: "#18181b", labelDir: "left" }
  ]}
/>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="Daniel Boone" value="Wilderness Road" helper="The frontiersman who became the symbol of westward settlement into Kentucky." />
  <TremorCard label="Indian Intercourse Act (1790)" value="Federal control" helper="Regulated trade with Native nations and required federal approval (treaties) for buying Native land. Settlers often ignored it." />
  <TremorCard label="Public Land Act (1796)" value="Orderly sales" helper="Set procedures for surveying and selling federal land at reasonable prices, building on the Land Ordinance of 1785." />
</div>

## 10.3 The cotton gin: a machine that expanded slavery

In **1793**, **Eli Whitney** built a simple machine, the cotton gin, that pulled the sticky seeds out of **short-staple cotton**, the kind that grows almost anywhere in the South. Before the gin, cleaning a single pound by hand took a day of labor. Follow the chain:

<div class="not-prose my-8 flex flex-wrap items-center justify-center gap-2 text-sm">
  <span class="rounded-lg border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/40 px-3 py-2 font-semibold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">⚙️ Cotton gin (1793)</span>
  <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold" aria-hidden="true">→</span>
  <span class="rounded-lg border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/40 px-3 py-2 font-semibold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">☁️ Short-staple cotton becomes profitable</span>
  <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold" aria-hidden="true">→</span>
  <span class="rounded-lg border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/40 px-3 py-2 font-semibold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">🌱 Plantations spread across the South and West</span>
  <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold" aria-hidden="true">→</span>
  <span class="rounded-lg border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/40 px-3 py-2 font-semibold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100">⛓️ Demand for enslaved labor rises</span>
</div>

<div class="not-prose my-6 grid sm:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <TremorCard label="U.S. cotton, 1790" value="~3,000 bales" helper="Cotton was a minor crop before the gin." />
  <TremorCard label="U.S. cotton, 1800" value="~73,000 bales" helper="More than twenty times as much, seven years after the gin. The boom was only beginning." />
</div>

<p class="not-prose text-sm font-semibold text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300 mt-6 mb-0">Enslaved people in the United States, by census (thousands)</p>

<RechartsBar
  data={[
    { census: "1790", enslaved: 698 },
    { census: "1800", enslaved: 894 },
    { census: "1810", enslaved: 1191 }
  ]}
  xKey="census"
  bars={[{ dataKey: "enslaved", name: "Enslaved people (thousands)", color: "#b91c1c" }]}
  showValues
  height={300}
/>

<Callout type="warning" title="The mistake graders see every year">
  The cotton gin did <strong>not</strong> reduce the need for enslaved workers. It made each enslaved person's labor in the fields more profitable, so planters bought more people and more land. By 1810 the enslaved population had grown by about 70 percent in twenty years.
</Callout>

## 10.4 Two regions, two directions

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">The North by 1800</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Gradual emancipation laws in every state from Pennsylvania north, a growing free Black population, and rising antislavery sentiment (Quakers were early leaders). Slavery was shrinking, slowly.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <h3 class="font-serif text-xl font-bold text-accent-900 dark:text-accent-100 mb-2">The South by 1800</h3>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200">Slavery more deeply entrenched and spreading into the Deep South and the lands west of the mountains, fueled by cotton. The regional difference that will dominate the next sixty years is now in place.</p>
  </div>
</div>

      ],
      answer: 0,
      explain: "Their portraits helped create a gallery of national heroes. Stuart's Washington is on the dollar bill."
    },
    {
      q: "Pierre Charles L'Enfant is associated with",
      choices: [
        "the first dictionary of American English",
        "the charter of the Bank of the United States",
        "the plan for Washington, D.C.",
        "the Treaty of Greenville with Native nations"
      ],
      answer: 2,
      explain: "His 1791 plan laid out grand avenues radiating from the Capitol and the President's House."
    },
    {
      q: "The Indian Intercourse Act of 1790 attempted to",
      choices: [
        "remove every Native nation to lands west of the Mississippi River",
        "regulate trade with Native nations and require federal approval of land sales",
        "grant full citizenship to all Native Americans living in the states",
        "open the entire Ohio country to settlement without any treaties"
      ],
      answer: 1,
      explain: "Land could be acquired only through federal treaties. In practice settlers kept pushing onto Native land anyway."
    },
    {
      q: "Which sequence correctly describes the effect of the cotton gin?",
      choices: [
        "Cotton gin, then fewer enslaved workers needed, then gradual emancipation in the South",
        "Cotton gin, then textile mills in Georgia, then a shift to free wage labor",
        "Cotton gin, then less land needed for cotton, then smaller southern plantations",
        "Cotton gin, then profitable cotton, then plantation expansion, then more slavery"
      ],
      answer: 3,
      explain: "Whitney's machine made short-staple cotton profitable, plantations spread west, and the enslaved population soared."
    },
    {
      q: "By 1800, regional attitudes toward slavery were best described as",
      choices: [
        "identical across all of the states because of the Constitution's many compromises",
        "hostile to slavery in the South while growing increasingly supportive in the North",
        "shifting toward emancipation in the North while slavery grew entrenched in the South",
        "unchanged since the colonial period in every single region of the new country"
      ],
      answer: 2,
      explain: "Northern gradual emancipation plus southern expansion created the sectional divide of the next century."
    }
  ]}
/>

# Section 11 · The Big Picture: Continuity and Change

<div class="not-prose my-8 rounded-2xl overflow-hidden bg-gradient-to-br from-ink-950 via-accent-950 to-ink-900 px-6 py-10 text-center">
  <p class="font-mono text-white/60 text-xs uppercase tracking-[0.25em] mb-3">Topic 3.13</p>
  <h2 class="font-serif text-3xl md:text-5xl font-bold text-white mb-2">How Revolutionary Was the Revolution?</h2>
  <p class="text-white/80 max-w-xl mx-auto">This topic pulls the whole unit together, and it is the most likely essay prompt from Period 3.</p>
</div>

## 11.1 The thesis that wins essays

If you get one essay on this period, this argument works for almost any prompt: **the American Revolution produced major political change but much more limited social change.**

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-accent-700 bg-accent-50 dark:bg-accent-950/30 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-800 dark:text-accent-300 mb-1">Politics</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-2xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">Transformed</h3>
    <div class="h-3 rounded-full bg-accent-200 dark:bg-accent-950 overflow-hidden mb-3" aria-hidden="true"><div class="h-3 w-[92%] bg-accent-700"></div></div>
    <ul class="space-y-1.5 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 list-disc list-inside">
      <li>Colonies became an independent nation</li>
      <li>Monarchy rejected for a republic</li>
      <li>Written constitutions with bills of rights</li>
      <li>A new federal system under the Constitution</li>
      <li>Organized political parties and a peaceful transfer of power</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border-2 border-ink-700 bg-ink-50 dark:bg-ink-950/40 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-ink-600 dark:text-ink-300 mb-1">Society</p>
    <h3 class="font-serif text-2xl font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-2">Partly changed</h3>
    <div class="h-3 rounded-full bg-ink-200 dark:bg-ink-800 overflow-hidden mb-3" aria-hidden="true"><div class="h-3 w-[35%] bg-ink-700 dark:bg-ink-300"></div></div>
    <ul class="space-y-1.5 text-sm text-ink-800 dark:text-ink-200 list-disc list-inside">
      <li>Slavery survived, and expanded in the South</li>
      <li>Women stayed excluded from politics</li>
      <li>Native nations kept losing land</li>
      <li>Voting still required property</li>
      <li>Economic inequality and British culture remained</li>
    </ul>
  </div>
</div>

<p class="not-prose -mt-4 mb-8 text-xs text-ink-500 dark:text-ink-400">The bars are an illustration of the argument, not measured data.</p>

## 11.2 What historians argue

AMSCO's "Historical Perspectives" boxes give you ready-made historiography. Knowing that historians disagree, and why, is how you earn the complexity point.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-3 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Early 1900s</p><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Progressive historians</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">The Revolution was a <strong>radical</strong> social as well as political revolution: federalism, equality, and individual rights. Charles Beard (1913) added that the framers wrote the Constitution partly to protect their own property.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Late 1900s</p><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Bernard Bailyn and others</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Representative government, wide voting, and written charters already existed in the colonies. The Revolution was <strong>evolutionary</strong>; what was new was a clear American philosophy of liberty.</p></div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5"><p class="font-mono text-[10px] uppercase tracking-widest font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-1">Comparative view</p><h3 class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Crane Brinton and critics</h3><p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Brinton saw the same stages as in the French and Russian revolutions. Critics answer that America had no feudal aristocracy to overthrow, so its revolution was far less radical.</p></div>
</div>

## 11.3 The 15 cause-and-effect links to know cold

If you are short on time, master these. Each one is a ready-made piece of essay evidence.

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 lg:grid-cols-3 gap-3 text-sm">
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">1</span> Seven Years' War <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> British debt <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> taxation</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">2</span> British taxation <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> colonial resistance</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">3</span> Intolerable Acts <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> colonial unity</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">4</span> Enlightenment <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> arguments for rights and independence</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">5</span> Common Sense <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> broader support for independence</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">6</span> Lexington and Concord <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> armed revolution</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">7</span> Saratoga <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> French alliance</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">8</span> French assistance <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> American victory</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">9</span> Revolutionary ideals <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> challenges to old social arrangements</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">10</span> Weak Articles <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> demands for a stronger government</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">11</span> Shays' Rebellion <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> support for constitutional reform</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">12</span> Anti-Federalist opposition <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> Bill of Rights</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">13</span> Hamilton's policies <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> political parties</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">14</span> French Revolution and European wars <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> foreign-policy and party conflict</div>
  <div class="rounded-xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-3"><span class="font-mono text-xs text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 font-bold">15</span> Cotton gin <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> more cotton <span class="text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400">→</span> more slavery</div>
</div>

Now test the links: match each cause to its effect.

<MatchPairs
  title="Cause to effect"
  pairs={[
    { term: "Seven Years' War debt", def: "Parliament taxes the colonies directly" },
    { term: "Intolerable Acts", def: "Twelve colonies meet in the First Continental Congress" },
    { term: "Common Sense", def: "Many undecided colonists turn toward independence" },
    { term: "Battle of Saratoga", def: "France openly allies with the United States" },
    { term: "Shays' Rebellion", def: "Leaders call for a stronger national government" },
    { term: "Anti-Federalist opposition", def: "The first ten amendments are added" },
    { term: "Hamilton's financial plan", def: "Jefferson and Madison organize an opposition party" },
    { term: "Alien and Sedition Acts", def: "The Kentucky and Virginia Resolutions" },
    { term: "The cotton gin", def: "Slavery expands across the Deep South" }
  ]}
/>

## 11.4 Articles vs Constitution

The single most important comparison in the unit. Know every row.

| | Articles of Confederation (1781-1789) | Constitution (1789 on) |
|---|---|---|
| Legislature | One house; one vote per state | Two houses: House by population, Senate equal |
| Executive | None | A president with a veto |
| National courts | None | Supreme Court and federal courts |
| Taxes | Could only ask states for money | Can lay and collect taxes |
| Trade | No power to regulate commerce | Regulates interstate and foreign commerce |
| Passing laws | 9 of 13 states | Majorities in both houses plus the president (or a 2/3 override) |
| Amending | All 13 states | 2/3 of Congress and 3/4 of the states |
| Where power sits | The states | Shared (federalism), with national law supreme |

---

# The primary source reader

Flip through the eight documents your test is most likely to quote. Each page ends with why it gets tested.

<Flipbook
  title="Period 3 in eight documents"
  attribution="Excerpts of standard Period 3 sources"
  cover={{ title: "Period 3 Sources", subtitle: "Eight documents your test loves", color: "#fee2e2" }}
  pages={[
    { content: "<h3>1 · Benjamin Franklin, Join, or Die, 1754</h3><p><em>A snake cut into eight pieces labeled with colony initials, over the words 'JOIN, or DIE.'</em></p><p><strong>Why it gets tested:</strong> the first famous call for colonial unity, made to promote the Albany Plan against the French. Reused during the Stamp Act crisis and in 1774 against Britain. Always check the date.</p>" },
    { content: "<h3>2 · Resolutions of the Stamp Act Congress, 1765</h3><p><em>'It is inseparably essential to the freedom of a People, and the undoubted Right of Englishmen, that no taxes be imposed on them, but with their own Consent, given personally, or by their representatives.'</em></p><p><strong>Why it gets tested:</strong> 'no taxation without representation' in official form, framed as the rights of <em>Englishmen</em>, from delegates who still professed loyalty to the king.</p>" },
    { content: "<h3>3 · John Dickinson, Letters from a Farmer in Pennsylvania, 1767-68</h3><p><em>'Those who are taxed without their own consent, expressed by themselves or their representatives, are slaves.'</em></p><p><strong>Why it gets tested:</strong> the moderate case against the Townshend duties. Parliament may regulate trade, but taxing for revenue without consent violates English liberty.</p>" },
    { content: "<h3>4 · Thomas Paine, Common Sense, January 1776</h3><p><em>'There is something very absurd, in supposing a continent to be perpetually governed by an island.'</em></p><p><strong>Why it gets tested:</strong> the shift from protesting Parliament to rejecting monarchy itself, in plain language for ordinary readers.</p>" },
    { content: "<h3>5 · Declaration of Independence, July 4, 1776</h3><p><em>'Governments are instituted among Men, deriving their just powers from the consent of the governed.'</em></p><p><strong>Why it gets tested:</strong> Locke's natural rights and social contract as the justification for revolution, and the equality claim that slavery contradicted.</p>" },
    { content: "<h3>6 · Abigail Adams to John Adams, March 31, 1776</h3><p><em>'Remember the Ladies, and be more generous and favourable to them than your ancestors. Do not put such unlimited power into the hands of the Husbands.'</em></p><p><strong>Why it gets tested:</strong> revolutionary logic applied to women, and a clear example of the limits of the Revolution's social change.</p>" },
    { content: "<h3>7 · James Madison, Federalist No. 10, 1787</h3><p><em>'Extend the sphere, and you take in a greater variety of parties and interests; you make it less probable that a majority of the whole will have a common motive to invade the rights of other citizens.'</em></p><p><strong>Why it gets tested:</strong> the Federalist answer to fears that a large republic would become tyrannical.</p>" },
    { content: "<h3>8 · George Washington, Farewell Address, 1796</h3><p><em>'It is our true policy to steer clear of permanent alliances with any portion of the foreign world.'</em></p><p><strong>Why it gets tested:</strong> the foundation of American foreign policy for over a century, plus warnings about parties and sectionalism that the 1790s were already ignoring.</p>" }
  ]}
/>

<Callout type="insight" title="Sourcing is half the answer">
  Every one of these is a primary source, but each has a point of view: a Patriot pamphleteer, a delegate who still loves the king, a wife writing privately, a retiring president shaping his legacy. Before you read a stimulus, read the sourcing line: <strong>who, when, to whom, and why</strong>.
</Callout>

# Test-taking machine

## Worked example: beating a stimulus question

Four steps, every time. Here they are on a real 1765 source that AMSCO uses.

<StepThrough
  title="Anatomy of a stimulus MCQ"
  steps={[
    {
      html: `<div style="padding:22px;max-width:580px;"><h3 style="font-family:serif;font-size:20px;margin-bottom:10px;color:#dc2626;">Step 1: Read the sourcing line FIRST</h3><p style="font-size:14px;line-height:1.55;font-style:italic;">'If the towns of Manchester and Birmingham, sending no representatives to Parliament, are notwithstanding there represented, why are not the cities of Albany and Boston equally represented in that Assembly?'</p><p style="font-size:14px;line-height:1.55;margin-top:6px;">Soame Jenyns, member of Parliament, 1765</p><p style="font-size:14px;line-height:1.55;margin-top:10px;">Before analyzing a word you know: a <strong>British MP</strong>, writing in <strong>1765</strong>, the year of the <strong>Stamp Act</strong>. He is defending Parliament.</p></div>`,
      caption: "Author + role + date = half the answer."
    },
    {
      html: `<div style="padding:22px;max-width:580px;"><h3 style="font-family:serif;font-size:20px;margin-bottom:10px;color:#dc2626;">Step 2: Ask what the quote is DOING</h3><p style="font-size:15px;line-height:1.55;">Manchester elects no one, yet counts as represented. So Boston is represented too. That is <strong>virtual representation</strong>: Parliament speaks for all British subjects, so it may tax them all.</p></div>`,
      caption: "Name the idea the author is using."
    },
    {
      html: `<div style="padding:22px;max-width:580px;"><h3 style="font-family:serif;font-size:20px;margin-bottom:10px;color:#dc2626;">Step 3: Connect it to the period</h3><p style="font-size:15px;line-height:1.55;">The likely question: 'Colonial leaders responded to arguments like this by...' The answer lives in 1765: the <strong>Stamp Act Congress</strong> resolved that only representatives the colonists <strong>actually elected</strong> could tax them. No taxation without representation.</p></div>`,
      caption: "Every stimulus plugs into a big process. Find the socket."
    },
    {
      html: `<div style="padding:22px;max-width:580px;"><h3 style="font-family:serif;font-size:20px;margin-bottom:10px;color:#dc2626;">Step 4: Kill choices by era and extremes</h3><p style="font-size:15px;line-height:1.55;">Wrong answers are usually <strong>right fact, wrong era</strong> (the Declaration of Independence is 1776, not a 1765 response), <strong>too extreme</strong> ('colonists immediately declared independence'), or the <strong>wrong side</strong> (Parliament's view presented as the colonists'). Eliminate by date first.</p></div>`,
      caption: "In 1765, almost no one wanted independence. Choices that say so are traps."
    }
  ]}
  height={330}
/>

## Practice: SAQ drills

Short answer questions have no thesis: answer A, B, and C in full sentences, do exactly what each verb asks, and back every claim with specific evidence. First decide whether the question tests **causation**, **comparison**, or **continuity and change**. Write your answer, then open the model.

<Collapsible question="Explain ONE way the British view of the colonies and ONE way the colonial view of Britain changed as a result of the Seven Years' War.">
  British view: Britain concluded that colonial militias were disorderly and that colonies were unwilling to pay or fight (some refused troops and money), so Britain decided to keep an army in America and make the colonists help pay for it through taxes like the Sugar and Stamp Acts. Colonial view: colonists grew proud and confident in their own military ability and unimpressed by British officers like Braddock, so they felt they no longer needed British protection once France was gone from the continent.
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible question="Explain ONE reason Britain issued the Proclamation of 1763, ONE reason colonists objected, and how effective it was.">
  Reason: after Pontiac's Rebellion, Britain wanted to prevent costly frontier wars by keeping settlers east of the Appalachians. Objection: colonists had fought the French and Indian War partly to win western land, and felt robbed of its prize by a distant government. Effectiveness: it largely failed, because thousands of settlers crossed the line anyway and Britain could not enforce it.
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible question="Explain how Enlightenment ideas influenced the Declaration of Independence.">
  Jefferson drew on John Locke: people have natural rights to life, liberty, and property (rephrased as 'Life, Liberty and the pursuit of Happiness'), governments exist by the consent of the governed to protect those rights, and when a government destroys them, the people have the right to alter or abolish it. The Declaration's list of grievances against George III serves as evidence that he broke the social contract, justifying revolution.
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible question="Explain TWO reasons the Americans won the Revolutionary War.">
  French assistance: after the victory at Saratoga (1777), France allied with the United States (1778), providing money, troops, and a navy; at Yorktown (1781) the French fleet blocked Cornwallis's escape by sea. British problems: Britain fought 3,000 miles from home with long supply lines, could capture cities but never control the vast countryside, and faced a growing world war and political opposition at home. (Washington's leadership in keeping the army alive also works.)
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible question="Explain ONE way the Revolution changed and ONE way it did not change the status of women OR African Americans.">
  Women: changed, because Republican Motherhood gave women a civic role in raising virtuous citizens and increased support for women's education; unchanged, because women still could not vote (except briefly in New Jersey) and Abigail Adams's plea to 'Remember the Ladies' was dismissed. African Americans: changed, because northern states began gradual emancipation (Pennsylvania 1780) and about 5,000 fought as Patriots; unchanged, because slavery survived and expanded in the South and was protected in the Constitution by the Three-Fifths Compromise.
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible question="Explain ONE achievement and ONE weakness of the Articles of Confederation, and how Shays' Rebellion led to the Constitution.">
  Achievement: the Northwest Ordinance (1787) created a process for territories to become equal states and banned slavery north of the Ohio River (or the Land Ordinance of 1785, or winning the war). Weakness: Congress had no power to tax, so it could not pay debts or fund an army. Shays' Rebellion (1786-87) showed the national government could not even protect a federal arsenal, which convinced leaders like Washington, Madison, and Hamilton to push for the 1787 Constitutional Convention.
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible question="Compare the Federalists and Democratic-Republicans of the 1790s on TWO issues.">
  The Constitution: Federalists like Hamilton favored loose construction and implied powers (the necessary and proper clause justified the national bank), while Democratic-Republicans like Jefferson favored strict construction. Foreign policy: Federalists leaned toward Britain, their main trading partner, while Democratic-Republicans sympathized with revolutionary France. (Also: the economy, where Federalists backed commerce, manufacturing, and tariffs and Democratic-Republicans backed agriculture.)
</Collapsible>

<Collapsible question="Compare Shays' Rebellion and the Whiskey Rebellion.">
  Similarity: both were revolts by indebted western farmers against taxes (state taxes in Massachusetts in 1786-87; the federal whiskey excise in Pennsylvania in 1794). Difference: under the Articles, the national government could do nothing about Shays' Rebellion, which exposed its weakness; under the Constitution, Washington federalized about 15,000 militiamen and ended the Whiskey Rebellion almost without bloodshed, proving the new government could enforce federal law.
</Collapsible>

          { name: "Neutrality, Jay, Pinckney, Whiskey" },
          { name: "Federalists vs Democratic-Republicans" },
          { name: "XYZ, Alien + Sedition, KY + VA, 1800" }
        ]
      },
      {
        name: "Identity + West (3.11-3.12)",
        children: [

          { name: "Boone, new states, Greenville" },
          { name: "Cotton gin 1793: slavery grows" }
        ]

    ]
  }}

# Mock unit test

Twenty-five questions, AP-paced. Set the timer, no notes, and run the four-step routine on every stimulus.

<TestTimer minutes={25} />

<Quiz
  title="Unit 3 mock test, 25 questions"
  questions={[
    { q: "Which development most directly caused Britain to end salutary neglect after 1763?", choices: ["The war debt and cost of defending territory won in the Seven Years' War", "The colonies' refusal to adopt Franklin's Albany Plan of Union in 1754", "A sharp decline in the colonial population in the years after the war", "French demands that Britain tax its colonies to pay war reparations"], answer: 0, explain: "Debt nearly doubled, and Britain chose to garrison about 10,000 soldiers in America. London concluded the colonists should help pay." },
    { q: "The Proclamation of 1763 was issued primarily in response to", choices: ["the Boston Tea Party", "Pontiac's Rebellion", "the Stamp Act Congress", "Shays' Rebellion"], answer: 1, explain: "Pontiac's alliance attacked forts and settlements in 1763; Britain drew the line at the Appalachians to prevent more wars." },
    { q: "Colonists objected to the Stamp Act more strongly than to earlier trade duties because it", choices: ["was collected by colonial assemblies rather than royal officials", "applied only to goods imported from the French West Indies", "was a direct tax paid by ordinary colonists on everyday items", "was proposed by colonial agents who lived in London"], answer: 2, explain: "Earlier duties were paid by merchants at the docks. Stamps hit anyone who bought a newspaper or signed a legal document." },
    { q: "'It is inseparably essential to the freedom of a People... that no taxes be imposed on them, but with their own Consent, given personally, or by their representatives.' This 1765 resolution most directly challenged the British idea of", choices: ["mercantilism", "salutary neglect", "divine right", "virtual representation"], answer: 3, explain: "Parliament claimed it virtually represented all British subjects. The Stamp Act Congress insisted only actually elected representatives could tax." },
    { q: "Which pair shows a British action followed by a colonial response to it?", choices: ["Boston Tea Party, then the Tea Act", "Declaration of Independence, then the Olive Branch Petition", "Townshend Acts, then nonimportation agreements", "First Continental Congress, then the Coercive Acts"], answer: 2, explain: "The other three pairs are in the wrong order." },
    { q: "The Election of 1800 is significant mainly because it", choices: ["was the first peaceful transfer of power between rival parties", "was decided by the Supreme Court after a tie in the House", "ended the political careers of both Jefferson and Burr", "was won by the Federalist candidate, John Adams"], answer: 0, explain: "Jefferson's 'Revolution of 1800': the Federalists gave up power without violence, proving the system could survive partisanship." },
    { q: "Paul Revere's engraving of the Boston Massacre is best understood as", choices: ["a neutral eyewitness record made for the soldiers' trial", "propaganda designed to stir anti-British feeling", "a British attempt to justify the soldiers' actions", "a Loyalist warning against mob violence in Boston"], answer: 1, explain: "The neat firing line, the 'Butcher's Hall' sign, and the passive crowd were all choices made to convict the soldiers in public opinion." },
    { q: "The Quebec Act (1774) alarmed many colonists because it", choices: ["gave French Canadians their own seats in the British Parliament", "banned the fur trade throughout the entire Great Lakes region", "returned Canada to French rule shortly after the war ended", "extended Quebec to the Ohio River without an elected assembly"], answer: 3, explain: "It took land colonists claimed, recognized Catholicism, and set up a government with no representative assembly." },
    { q: "The phrase 'deriving their just powers from the consent of the governed' most directly reflects the ideas of", choices: ["Thomas Hobbes", "Adam Smith", "John Locke", "Jonathan Edwards"], answer: 2, explain: "Locke's social contract: legitimate government rests on the consent of the people it governs." },
    { q: "Thomas Paine's Common Sense differed from earlier colonial protests because it", choices: ["attacked monarchy itself and demanded independence", "asked Parliament to give the colonies representation", "defended the king against his corrupt ministers", "called for a return to the Albany Plan of Union"], answer: 0, explain: "Earlier protests blamed Parliament and professed loyalty to the king. Paine rejected kingship altogether." },
    { q: "The most important result of the Battle of Saratoga was", choices: ["the immediate end of the Revolutionary War in the North", "Washington's recapture of New York City from the British", "Britain's decision to evacuate its troops from Canada", "a formal alliance between France and the United States"], answer: 3, explain: "The victory convinced France to ally openly with the Americans in 1778." },
    { q: "Eli Whitney's cotton gin (1793) contributed to", choices: ["a steep decline in the number of enslaved people in the South", "the expansion of cotton plantations and of slavery", "the end of the Atlantic slave trade that same year", "the rise of large textile factories in the Deep South"], answer: 1, explain: "Profitable short-staple cotton spread plantations west, and the enslaved population grew rapidly." },
    { q: "Republican Motherhood encouraged", choices: ["women's right to vote in state elections", "women serving as soldiers in the Continental Army", "the education of women to raise virtuous citizens", "women holding seats in the state legislatures"], answer: 2, explain: "A civic role without political rights: mothers would raise republican sons, so women needed education." },
    { q: "Which statement best describes the effect of the Revolution on slavery?", choices: ["Northern states began gradual emancipation while slavery persisted in the South", "Slavery was abolished nationwide under the terms of the Treaty of Paris of 1783", "The Articles of Confederation outlawed slavery in every one of the states", "Slavery grew fastest in the New England states during the 1780s and 1790s"], answer: 0, explain: "Pennsylvania (1780) led northern gradual emancipation; the South held on to slavery and soon expanded it." },
    { q: "The greatest achievement of the government under the Articles of Confederation was the", choices: ["Commerce Compromise", "Proclamation of Neutrality", "Judiciary Act of 1789", "Northwest Ordinance of 1787"], answer: 3, explain: "A process for new, equal states and a ban on slavery north of the Ohio River. The other three came under the Constitution." },
    { q: "Shays' Rebellion (1786-1787) is significant because it", choices: ["led directly to Parliament's repeal of the much-hated Stamp Act", "exposed the weakness of the national government under the Articles", "was the first real battle to be fought in the Revolutionary War", "convinced Massachusetts to withdraw from the Confederation entirely"], answer: 1, explain: "Congress could not raise money or troops to protect even a federal arsenal, and calls for a new constitution grew louder." },
    { q: "The Great Compromise at the Constitutional Convention created", choices: ["a one-house Congress in which each state received an equal vote", "a president elected directly by a national popular vote", "a two-house Congress: one house by population, one with equal votes", "a Supreme Court whose justices were chosen by the state legislatures"], answer: 2, explain: "House by population (large states), Senate with two per state (small states)." },
    { q: "Anti-Federalists feared that the Constitution would", choices: ["create a central government powerful enough to threaten liberty", "leave the national government too weak to pay its debts", "abolish slavery immediately in all of the southern states", "give small states more power than large states in Congress"], answer: 0, explain: "Having fought a distant, powerful government, they feared another one, especially without a bill of rights." },
    { q: "Madison's argument in Federalist No. 10 was that", choices: ["only small republics could protect individual liberty", "political parties should be banned by the Constitution", "state governments should keep most political power", "a large republic makes it harder for one faction to dominate"], answer: 3, explain: "Many competing interests in a large republic make an oppressive majority less likely." },
    { q: "The Bill of Rights was added to the Constitution mainly to", choices: ["expand the powers of the new federal government", "win over Anti-Federalists who feared federal tyranny", "end the importation of enslaved people before 1808", "settle the dispute between the large and small states"], answer: 1, explain: "Federalists promised amendments to secure ratification, and Madison drafted them in 1789; ten were ratified in 1791." },
    { q: "Alexander Hamilton's financial program included all of the following EXCEPT", choices: ["federal assumption of the states' war debts", "a national bank chartered by Congress", "the elimination of all tariffs on imports", "an excise tax on whiskey"], answer: 2, explain: "Hamilton wanted tariffs, both for revenue and to protect infant industries." },
    { q: "The dispute between Hamilton and Jefferson over the national bank centered on", choices: ["how strictly or loosely to interpret the Constitution", "whether the United States should declare war on France", "whether new states could join the Union as equals", "how many justices should sit on the Supreme Court"], answer: 0, explain: "Loose construction and implied powers (Hamilton) vs strict construction (Jefferson)." },
    { q: "The Whiskey Rebellion (1794) differed from Shays' Rebellion in that", choices: ["it was led mostly by veterans of the Revolutionary War", "it began over taxes that struggling western farmers resented", "it broke out in the western, frontier part of a state", "the national government quickly put it down with militia"], answer: 3, explain: "Both were western farmers' tax revolts. The difference was the response: the new federal government could act; the Articles government could not." },
    { q: "Washington's Farewell Address advised the United States to", choices: ["join permanent alliances with other free republics", "avoid permanent alliances and the spirit of party", "build a large navy to protect American merchants", "support revolutionary France in its war on Britain"], answer: 1, explain: "Commerce with all, permanent alliances with none, and beware of parties and sectionalism." },
    { q: "The Kentucky and Virginia Resolutions (1798) introduced the argument that", choices: ["the Supreme Court can strike down any act passed by Congress", "Congress may limit the freedom of the press during wartime", "states may nullify federal laws they judge unconstitutional", "the president may deport immigrants without any hearing"], answer: 2, explain: "Jefferson and Madison's compact theory, a response to the Alien and Sedition Acts, later used in the nullification crisis." }
  ]}
