## Flashcards: the full deck

Signed in, these schedule themselves with spaced repetition and feed your daily review queue.

<Flashcards
  cards={[
    { front: "Seven Years' War / French and Indian War", back: "1754-1763. Fourth British-French war for empire; began in the Ohio Valley. Britain won, but its debt nearly doubled." },
    { front: "Albany Plan of Union (1754)", back: "Franklin's plan for an intercolonial government for defense and taxes. Rejected (colonies guarded their taxing power); precedent for later congresses." },
    { front: "Join, or Die (1754)", back: "Franklin's snake cartoon for the Albany Plan: unite or be destroyed. Reused later against Britain." },
    { front: "Treaty of Paris (1763)", back: "Britain gains French Canada and Spanish Florida; France gives Louisiana to Spain. French power in North America ends." },
    { front: "British vs colonial views after the war", back: "British: colonial militias were a rabble; colonists should pay. Colonists: proud, confident, unimpressed by British officers." },
    { front: "Salutary neglect (end of)", back: "Britain's lax enforcement ended after 1763: trade laws enforced, revenue taxes passed, army kept in America." },
    { front: "Pontiac's Rebellion (1763)", back: "Ottawa chief Pontiac's alliance attacked frontier forts; Britain used regulars. Led to the Proclamation of 1763." },
    { front: "Proclamation of 1763", back: "No colonial settlement west of the Appalachians. Angered colonists; widely ignored." },
    { front: "Sugar Act (1764)", back: "Revenue Act: duties on foreign sugar and luxuries; smugglers tried in juryless admiralty courts." },
    { front: "Quartering Act (1765)", back: "Colonies must house and feed British troops. Expanded in 1774; answered by the 3rd Amendment." },
    { front: "Stamp Act (1765)", back: "First DIRECT tax: stamps on newspapers, legal documents, pamphlets. Repealed 1766 after boycotts." },
    { front: "Stamp Act Congress (1765)", back: "Nine colonies; loyal to the king, but only elected representatives may tax them." },
    { front: "Sons and Daughters of Liberty", back: "Protest groups: Sons intimidated tax collectors; Daughters made homespun to power boycotts." },
    { front: "Virtual representation", back: "Parliament's claim to represent all British subjects, including unrepresented colonists. Rejected by colonists." },
    { front: "Declaratory Act (1766)", back: "Parliament may legislate for the colonies 'in all cases whatsoever.' Passed with the Stamp Act's repeal." },
    { front: "Townshend Acts (1767)", back: "Duties on tea, glass, paper, lead, paint; revenue paid royal officials; writs of assistance. Repealed 1770 except tea." },
    { front: "Letters from a Farmer in Pennsylvania", back: "John Dickinson (1767-68): Parliament may regulate trade, but revenue duties without consent are illegal." },
    { front: "Massachusetts Circular Letter (1768)", back: "Samuel Adams and James Otis urged all colonies to petition against the Townshend Acts. Britain sent troops to Boston." },
    { front: "Boston Massacre (1770)", back: "Soldiers killed five, including Crispus Attucks. John Adams defended them; Samuel Adams and Revere used it as propaganda." },
    { front: "Committees of Correspondence (1772)", back: "Samuel Adams's letter network linking towns, then colonies. Built intercolonial unity." },
    { front: "Tea Act and Boston Tea Party (1773)", back: "Tea Act made taxed tea cheaper; accepting it meant accepting Parliament's power. Bostonians dumped 342 chests." },
    { front: "Coercive (Intolerable) Acts (1774)", back: "Port Act, Massachusetts Government Act, Administration of Justice Act, expanded quartering. Plus the Quebec Act. United the colonies." },
    { front: "Locke's ideas", back: "Natural rights (life, liberty, property), social contract, consent of the governed, right of revolution." },
    { front: "Republicanism", back: "Government by elected representatives, civic virtue, opposition to monarchy and concentrated power." },
    { front: "Common Sense (1776)", back: "Thomas Paine: plain-language attack on monarchy; 'a continent... governed by an island' is absurd. Boosted support for independence." },
    { front: "First Continental Congress (1774)", back: "All colonies but Georgia. Suffolk Resolves, Declaration and Resolves, Continental Association boycott. Not yet independence." },
    { front: "Lexington and Concord (1775)", back: "April 1775: Gage's troops seize supplies; Revere and Dawes warn; first shots; British take 250 casualties on the retreat." },
    { front: "Bunker (Breed's) Hill (1775)", back: "British take the hill but lose over 1,000 men. Proved militia could fight regulars." },
    { front: "Second Continental Congress", back: "May 1775 onward: Continental Army, Washington in command, Olive Branch Petition, then independence." },
    { front: "Olive Branch Petition (1775)", back: "Final plea of loyalty to George III. Rejected; colonies declared in rebellion." },
    { front: "Declaration of Independence", back: "Adopted July 4, 1776 (independence voted July 2). Jefferson: Locke's theory, grievances against George III, formal break." },
    { front: "Loyalists (Tories)", back: "20 to 30 percent; majority in NY, NJ, GA; wealthier, officials, Anglican clergy. About 80,000 left after the war." },
    { front: "Saratoga (1777)", back: "Burgoyne surrenders; France allies with the U.S. in 1778. The turning point." },
    { front: "Yorktown (1781)", back: "Washington and the French trap Cornwallis; the French fleet blocks escape. Effectively ends the war." },
    { front: "Treaty of Paris (1783)", back: "Independence; Mississippi River western border; fishing rights off Canada; pay British debts and honor Loyalist claims." },
    { front: "Republican Motherhood", back: "Women serve the republic by raising virtuous citizens; boosted women's education, not women's rights." },
    { front: "Articles of Confederation", back: "Adopted 1777, ratified 1781. One-house Congress, one vote per state, no executive, no courts, no taxing power." },
    { front: "Land Ordinance of 1785", back: "Surveyed western land into 6-by-6-mile townships of 36 sections; section 16 for public schools." },
    { front: "Northwest Ordinance (1787)", back: "Territory to statehood (5,000 men: legislature; 60,000 people: statehood on equal footing). Banned slavery north of the Ohio." },
    { front: "Shays' Rebellion (1786-87)", back: "Indebted Massachusetts farmers closed courts and attacked the Springfield arsenal. Showed the Articles were too weak." },
    { front: "Constitutional Convention (1787)", back: "Philadelphia, May to September; 55 delegates, no Rhode Island; Washington presiding; Madison the architect." },
    { front: "Great (Connecticut) Compromise", back: "Roger Sherman: House by population, Senate with two per state." },
    { front: "Three-Fifths Compromise", back: "Enslaved people counted as 3/5 of a person for representation and direct taxes." },
    { front: "Commerce Compromise", back: "Congress regulates interstate and foreign trade and may tax imports, never exports." },
    { front: "Federalists vs Anti-Federalists", back: "For ratification and strong government (Hamilton, Madison, Jay) vs against it without a bill of rights (Mason, Henry)." },
    { front: "The Federalist Papers", back: "85 essays by Hamilton, Madison, and Jay ('Publius'). No. 10: a large republic controls factions." },
    { front: "Bill of Rights (1791)", back: "First ten amendments, drafted by Madison. 10th: undelegated powers reserved to the states or the people." },
    { front: "Washington's cabinet", back: "Jefferson (State), Hamilton (Treasury), Knox (War), Randolph (Attorney General)." },
    { front: "Hamilton's financial plan", back: "Pay debt at face value and assume state debts; protective tariffs; Bank of the United States; whiskey excise." },
    { front: "Loose vs strict construction", back: "Loose (Hamilton): necessary and proper clause allows implied powers like a bank. Strict (Jefferson): only listed powers." },
    { front: "Proclamation of Neutrality (1793)", back: "Washington keeps the U.S. out of the war between France and Britain." },
    { front: "Jay's Treaty (1794)", back: "Britain leaves its western forts; nothing on impressment. Unpopular, but kept the peace." },
    { front: "Pinckney's Treaty (1795)", back: "Spain opens the Mississippi and New Orleans (right of deposit); Florida border at the 31st parallel." },
    { front: "Whiskey Rebellion (1794)", back: "Pennsylvania farmers resist the whiskey tax; Washington sends about 15,000 militia. Federal power proven." },
    { front: "Fallen Timbers and Greenville", back: "1794: Wayne defeats the Western Confederacy. 1795: Native nations cede most of Ohio." },
    { front: "Farewell Address (1796)", back: "Avoid permanent alliances, political parties, and sectionalism. Two-term precedent." },
    { front: "XYZ Affair (1797-98)", back: "French agents demand bribes; 'Millions for defense, but not one cent for tribute'; undeclared Quasi-War." },
    { front: "Alien and Sedition Acts (1798)", back: "Citizenship wait 5 to 14 years; deport 'dangerous' aliens; crime to criticize the government." },
    { front: "Kentucky and Virginia Resolutions (1798)", back: "Jefferson (KY) and Madison (VA): the compact theory; states may nullify unconstitutional federal laws." },
    { front: "Election of 1800", back: "Jefferson and Burr tie at 73; House picks Jefferson on the 36th ballot with Hamilton's help. 12th Amendment follows (1804)." },
    { front: "Cotton gin (1793)", back: "Eli Whitney. Short-staple cotton becomes profitable; plantations and slavery expand." },
    { front: "Peale, Stuart, L'Enfant", back: "Peale and Stuart painted national heroes (Stuart's Washington is on the $1 bill); L'Enfant planned Washington, D.C." }
  ]}
/>

## Mnemonics

<div class="not-prose my-8 grid md:grid-cols-2 gap-4">
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[10px] font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-2">The acts, in order (1763-1774)</p>
    <p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Please Stop Stamping Quarters; Don't Toss Tea, Colonists</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300"><strong>P</strong>roclamation, <strong>S</strong>ugar, <strong>S</strong>tamp, <strong>Q</strong>uartering, <strong>D</strong>eclaratory, <strong>T</strong>ownshend, <strong>T</strong>ea, <strong>C</strong>oercive.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[10px] font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-2">Locke's rights</p>
    <p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">L-L-P becomes L-L-H</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Locke: <strong>L</strong>ife, <strong>L</strong>iberty, <strong>P</strong>roperty. Jefferson swaps the P for <strong>H</strong>appiness (the pursuit of it).</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[10px] font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-2">Turning point</p>
    <p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Saratoga Summons France</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">1777 win, 1778 alliance. And at the end, the French fleet makes Yorktown work.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[10px] font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-2">Treaty of Paris, 1783</p>
    <p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">I Must Fish Daily</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300"><strong>I</strong>ndependence, <strong>M</strong>ississippi border, <strong>F</strong>ishing rights, <strong>D</strong>ebts (and Loyalist property).</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[10px] font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-2">What the Articles could NOT do</p>
    <p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">No T-C-E</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">No <strong>T</strong>axes, no <strong>C</strong>ommerce regulation, no <strong>E</strong>xecutive to enforce laws.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[10px] font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-2">The convention's compromises</p>
    <p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Great Things Come</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300"><strong>G</strong>reat (representation), <strong>T</strong>hree-Fifths (slavery), <strong>C</strong>ommerce (trade).</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[10px] font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-2">Two rebellions</p>
    <p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">Shays = Shaky, Whiskey = Whipped</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Shays showed a <strong>shaky</strong> government under the Articles; the Whiskey rebels got <strong>whipped</strong> by the new federal government.</p>
  </div>
  <div class="rounded-2xl border border-ink-200 dark:border-ink-800 bg-white dark:bg-ink-900 p-5">
    <p class="font-mono uppercase tracking-[0.18em] text-[10px] font-bold text-accent-700 dark:text-accent-400 mb-2">Farewell Address</p>
    <p class="font-serif text-lg font-bold text-ink-900 dark:text-ink-100 mb-1">No Parties, No Pacts, No Sections</p>
    <p class="text-sm text-ink-700 dark:text-ink-300">Beware political <strong>parties</strong>, permanent alliances (<strong>pacts</strong>), and <strong>sectionalism</strong>.</p>
  </div>
</div>

## Common pitfalls

<BadFact source="patriotic memory" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 1">
  Bunker Hill was the first big American victory.
</BadFact>

The British **took** the hill. It was a British tactical win at a terrible price (over 1,000 casualties). Americans called it "a victory of sorts" because they proved they could fight regulars. On the test, "British victory with heavy losses" is the right description.

<BadFact source="date drift" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 2">
  The Articles of Confederation were ratified in 1777.
</BadFact>

Congress **adopted** the Articles in 1777, but they were not **ratified** until **1781**, because Maryland held out until states with western land claims gave them up.

<BadFact source="signed vs ratified" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 3">
  The Constitution was ratified on September 17, 1787.
</BadFact>

September 17, 1787 is when the convention **signed** it and sent it to the states. It was **ratified** when New Hampshire became the ninth state on **June 21, 1788**. The new government began in **1789**, and Rhode Island did not ratify until **May 1790**.

<BadFact source="the elastic clause" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 4">
  Hamilton created the "necessary and proper" clause to justify the bank.
</BadFact>

The clause was already in Article I, Section 8, written by the convention in 1787. Hamilton **interpreted** it loosely to claim implied powers. The skill being tested is loose vs strict **construction**, not who wrote the clause.

<BadFact source="price confusion" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 5">
  The Boston Tea Party protested a tax increase that made tea more expensive.
</BadFact>

The Tea Act of 1773 made East India Company tea **cheaper**. Colonists destroyed it because buying it meant accepting Parliament's right to tax them. The protest was about **principle**, not price.

<BadFact source="oversimplified history" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 6">
  Washington's Farewell Address told Americans to avoid all contact with foreign nations.
</BadFact>

He urged **trade** with every nation and **permanent political alliances** with none. Isolationism is a later caricature of his point.

<BadFact source="the most common APUSH error" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 7">
  The cotton gin reduced the need for enslaved labor.
</BadFact>

The opposite. It made cotton so profitable that plantations spread west and the enslaved population grew by about 70 percent between 1790 and 1810.

<BadFact source="lazy essays" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 8">
  The Articles of Confederation accomplished nothing.
</BadFact>

The Articles government won the war, negotiated the Treaty of Paris (1783), and passed the Land Ordinance (1785) and the Northwest Ordinance (1787), which set the pattern for every new state. Weak, yes. Useless, no.

<BadFact source="1776 nostalgia" certainty="0%" title="MISCONCEPTION 9">
  Nearly all colonists supported independence.
</BadFact>

Roughly 40 percent were Patriots, 20 to 30 percent were Loyalists, and the rest tried to stay out of it. The Revolution was also a civil war.

### Dates worth knowing

| Year | Event | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| 1754 | French and Indian War begins; Albany Plan | The war that changes everything; first try at unity |
| 1763 | Treaty of Paris; Proclamation of 1763 | Britain wins the continent, then blocks the West |
| 1765 | Stamp Act; Stamp Act Congress | First direct tax; united protest |
| 1767 | Townshend Acts | Indirect taxes, writs of assistance, new boycotts |
| 1770 | Boston Massacre | Five killed; a propaganda gift |
| 1773 | Tea Act; Boston Tea Party | Principle over price |
| 1774 | Intolerable Acts; First Continental Congress | Punishment unites the colonies |
| 1775 | Lexington and Concord; Bunker Hill; Olive Branch | War begins while Congress still asks for peace |
| 1776 | Common Sense; Declaration of Independence | Independence |
| 1777 | Saratoga | Turning point; French alliance in 1778 |
| 1781 | Yorktown; Articles ratified | Victory; the first national government |
| 1783 | Treaty of Paris | Independence and the Mississippi border |
| 1785 | Land Ordinance | Township grid, section 16 for schools |
| 1786-87 | Shays' Rebellion | Proof the Articles failed |
| 1787 | Constitutional Convention; Northwest Ordinance | New Constitution; path to statehood, no slavery in the NW |
| 1788 | Constitution ratified | New Hampshire is the ninth state |
| 1789 | Washington takes office; Judiciary Act | Precedents and federal courts |
| 1791 | Bill of Rights; Bank of the United States | Rights protected; loose construction wins |
| 1793 | Neutrality Proclamation; cotton gin | Stay out of Europe; slavery's future expands |
| 1794 | Whiskey Rebellion; Fallen Timbers; Jay's Treaty | Federal power; the Ohio country opened |
| 1795 | Pinckney's Treaty; Treaty of Greenville | The Mississippi opened; Native land ceded |
| 1796 | Farewell Address | No permanent alliances, no parties |
| 1798 | XYZ fallout; Alien and Sedition Acts; KY + VA Resolutions | Free speech vs security; nullification |
| 1800 | Election of 1800 | Peaceful transfer of power |

### Shays vs Whiskey

| | Shays' Rebellion | Whiskey Rebellion |
|---|---|---|
| When | 1786-1787 | 1794 |
| Where | Western Massachusetts | Western Pennsylvania |
| Against | High state taxes and debtors' courts | The federal excise tax on whiskey |
| Government | Articles of Confederation | Constitution |
| Response | National government helpless; privately funded state militia | Washington sends about 15,000 militiamen |
| Lesson | The national government is too weak | The new federal government can enforce its laws |

### Federalists vs Democratic-Republicans, in one line each

**Federalists** (Hamilton, Adams): strong central government, loose construction, bank and tariffs, commerce, pro-British, northern merchants. **Democratic-Republicans** (Jefferson, Madison): states' rights, strict construction, against the bank, agriculture, pro-French, farmers of the South and West.

## Short answer (AI graded)

Write real paragraphs in your own words. The tutor scores each out of 5 and tells you what a grader would dock.

<ShortAnswer
  title="Unit 3 short answer"
  questions={[
    {
      q: "Explain how the outcome of the French and Indian War led to conflict between Britain and its colonies. Use at least TWO specific British actions as evidence.",
      rubric: "Causation. The war left Britain with a nearly doubled debt and a larger empire to defend, so Britain ended salutary neglect and made colonists help pay. Evidence (any two, explained): Proclamation of 1763 (blocked western settlement after Pontiac's Rebellion), Sugar Act (revenue duties, admiralty courts), Quartering Act, Stamp Act (first direct tax, provoked the Stamp Act Congress and boycotts), Declaratory Act, Townshend Acts. Strong answers note the opposite British and colonial views of each other after the war, and the colonial argument of no taxation without representation versus virtual representation.",
      points: 5,
    },
    {
      q: "Explain how Enlightenment ideas shaped the colonists' decision to declare independence.",
      rubric: "Locke's natural rights (life, liberty, property), the social contract, consent of the governed, and the right of revolution; republicanism. Evidence: the Declaration of Independence's language (unalienable rights; Life, Liberty and the pursuit of Happiness; just powers from the consent of the governed; right to alter or abolish), and Paine's Common Sense attacking monarchy itself. Strong answers explain that the grievance list was evidence George III broke the social contract, and may note the contradiction with slavery.",
      points: 5,
    },
    {
      q: "Evaluate the extent to which the American Revolution changed American society between 1775 and 1800.",
      rubric: "Needs a claim of extent (for example: politics changed greatly, society much less). Change: independence, republican state constitutions with bills of rights, abolition of titles and primogeniture, disestablishment of churches (Virginia Statute for Religious Freedom), northern gradual emancipation, Republican Motherhood and women's education. Continuity: slavery persisted and expanded (Three-Fifths Compromise, cotton gin), women could not vote (Abigail Adams dismissed), property requirements for voting, Native Americans lost land. Credit historiography (Progressive radical view vs Bailyn's evolutionary view).",
      points: 5,
    },
    {
      q: "Explain why the Constitution replaced the Articles of Confederation, and how the Constitution addressed the Articles' weaknesses.",
      rubric: "Weaknesses: no power to tax, no power to regulate commerce, no executive, no national courts, unanimity to amend; foreign disrespect (British forts, Spanish Mississippi); Shays' Rebellion showed the government could not act. Constitution's answers: taxing power, the Commerce Clause, a president with a veto, a federal judiciary, an amendment process of two thirds and three quarters, federalism with national law supreme, balanced by separation of powers and checks and balances; the Bill of Rights to reassure Anti-Federalists. Strong answers contrast Shays' Rebellion with the Whiskey Rebellion.",
      points: 5,
    }
  ]}
/>
